# Enterprise AI Use-Case Compendium
_Prepared July 14, 2026._
## Executive orientation
A **use case** is the business problem being solved, stated independently of any particular way of solving it; a **methodology** is a reusable arrangement of AI methods; and a **solution** is a methodology applied correctly in context and wired to a business action. This compendium therefore catalogs the problem layer: the recurring enterprise decisions, economic pools, data exhaust, action systems, prerequisites, prevalence, results, and failure modes.
The headline is that value concentrates where four conditions coincide: high-frequency decisions, measurable economic consequences, available data exhaust, and clear action authority. McKinsey estimated that generative AI alone could add $2.6 trillion to $4.4 trillion annually across 63 use cases, with about 75% of value concentrated in customer operations, marketing and sales, software engineering, and R&D; Stanford HAI reported corporate AI investment of $252.3 billion in 2024; and McKinsey’s 2025 survey found 88% regular AI use in at least one business function but only about one-third scaling enterprise-wide. [McKinsey 2023 generative AI economic-potential report](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/tech-and-ai/our-insights/the-economic-potential-of-generative-ai-the-next-productivity-frontier); [Stanford HAI AI Index 2025](https://hai.stanford.edu/ai-index/2025-ai-index-report/economy); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai)
The adoption reality-check is sobering: BCG reported in 2024 that only 26% of companies had moved beyond proofs of concept to tangible value, while a 2020 MIT Sloan/BCG study found only 10% of companies obtained significant financial benefits from AI. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/)
## Full dossiers by business function

## A. Customer / revenue

### Churn prediction & retention

1. **Name + aliases** — Churn prediction & retention; aliases include churn, attrition, cancellation, lapse, logo churn, subscriber churn, merchant attrition.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is customers or accounts leaving, downgrading, reducing spend, or failing to renew. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: customer. Outcome owner: chief revenue officer, head of growth, customer success, product, ecommerce, or pricing leader.
4. **Economic pool** — Retention and revenue-protection value is high: Bain reported that a 5% retention-rate increase can raise profits by 25% to 95%, while McKinsey cites next-best-experience programs with 5% to 8% revenue lift and 20% to 30% cost-to-serve reduction. [Bain Loyalty Rules](https://www.bain.com/contentassets/29f74ec417fa4e36a1d7d7e7479badc5/loyalty_rules_chapter_one.pdf); [McKinsey next-best-experience case examples](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/growth-marketing-and-sales/our-insights/next-best-experience-how-ai-can-power-every-customer-interaction)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **account/subscriber/merchant**. Decision: **retain, save, recover, or let churn**. Label/outcome: **churn/renewal/spend decline**. Horizon: **7 days to 12 months**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: CRM, CDP, product analytics, billing/subscription, commerce, support, marketing automation, data warehouse. In practice this data often exists but needs cleaning; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to retain, save, recover, or let churn; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are churn/renewal/spend decline, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium-high: data usually exists but requires integration, standard definitions, and human-in-the-loop workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — SaaS defines churn at account or logo renewal; telecom and banking define it at subscriber/account level; retail often treats it as repeat purchase and spend decay; marketplaces treat it as merchant or buyer dormancy.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to personalization, offer optimization, customer service, pricing, and marketing attribution; the boundary is the business decision being changed, not the model feature set.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in scaled programs and advanced pilots: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: McKinsey reported a global payments processor could reduce merchant attrition by up to 20% per year, and Carrefour increased LTV ROAS of newly acquired customers by 21% using an LTV model. [McKinsey next-best-experience case examples](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/growth-marketing-and-sales/our-insights/next-best-experience-how-ai-can-power-every-customer-interaction); [Google/Carrefour LTV case study](https://services.google.com/fh/files/blogs/carrefour_case_study.pdf)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are unclear intervention economics, leakage from treatment into labels, uncoordinated channels, privacy/consent gaps, and optimizing short-term conversion while harming long-term value; BCG and MIT/BCG both show scaling value depends more on organizational learning and adoption capabilities than model availability alone. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Classification: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [Bain Loyalty Rules](https://www.bain.com/contentassets/29f74ec417fa4e36a1d7d7e7479badc5/loyalty_rules_chapter_one.pdf); [McKinsey next-best-experience case examples](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/growth-marketing-and-sales/our-insights/next-best-experience-how-ai-can-power-every-customer-interaction); [Google/Carrefour LTV case study](https://services.google.com/fh/files/blogs/carrefour_case_study.pdf); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Customer lifetime value

1. **Name + aliases** — Customer lifetime value; aliases include CLV, LTV, customer value, account value, predicted future margin.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which customers will generate high future gross margin or strategic value. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: customer. Outcome owner: chief revenue officer, head of growth, customer success, product, ecommerce, or pricing leader.
4. **Economic pool** — Retention and revenue-protection value is high: Bain reported that a 5% retention-rate increase can raise profits by 25% to 95%, while McKinsey cites next-best-experience programs with 5% to 8% revenue lift and 20% to 30% cost-to-serve reduction. [Bain Loyalty Rules](https://www.bain.com/contentassets/29f74ec417fa4e36a1d7d7e7479badc5/loyalty_rules_chapter_one.pdf); [McKinsey next-best-experience case examples](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/growth-marketing-and-sales/our-insights/next-best-experience-how-ai-can-power-every-customer-interaction)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **customer/account/household**. Decision: **acquire, invest, tier, suppress, or prioritize**. Label/outcome: **future net revenue/margin**. Horizon: **3 months to lifetime**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from thousands to hundreds of thousands of daily or weekly decisions; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: CRM, CDP, product analytics, billing/subscription, commerce, support, marketing automation, data warehouse. In practice this data commonly exists in usable form; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to acquire, invest, tier, suppress, or prioritize; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are future net revenue/margin, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — High: data and labels are usually captured in operational systems, and the action can be wired into existing workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — SaaS defines churn at account or logo renewal; telecom and banking define it at subscriber/account level; retail often treats it as repeat purchase and spend decay; marketplaces treat it as merchant or buyer dormancy.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to personalization, offer optimization, customer service, pricing, and marketing attribution; the boundary is the business decision being changed, not the model feature set.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is deployed at scale in many data-mature enterprises: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: McKinsey reported a global payments processor could reduce merchant attrition by up to 20% per year, and Carrefour increased LTV ROAS of newly acquired customers by 21% using an LTV model. [McKinsey next-best-experience case examples](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/growth-marketing-and-sales/our-insights/next-best-experience-how-ai-can-power-every-customer-interaction); [Google/Carrefour LTV case study](https://services.google.com/fh/files/blogs/carrefour_case_study.pdf)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are unclear intervention economics, leakage from treatment into labels, uncoordinated channels, privacy/consent gaps, and optimizing short-term conversion while harming long-term value; BCG and MIT/BCG both show scaling value depends more on organizational learning and adoption capabilities than model availability alone. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Regression: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [Bain Loyalty Rules](https://www.bain.com/contentassets/29f74ec417fa4e36a1d7d7e7479badc5/loyalty_rules_chapter_one.pdf); [McKinsey next-best-experience case examples](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/growth-marketing-and-sales/our-insights/next-best-experience-how-ai-can-power-every-customer-interaction); [Google/Carrefour LTV case study](https://services.google.com/fh/files/blogs/carrefour_case_study.pdf); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Next-best-action / next-best-offer

1. **Name + aliases** — Next-best-action / next-best-offer; aliases include NBA, NBO, next-best-experience, recommendation-to-act.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is what action, message, offer, or service step should be taken for a customer now. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: customer. Outcome owner: chief revenue officer, head of growth, customer success, product, ecommerce, or pricing leader.
4. **Economic pool** — Retention and revenue-protection value is high: Bain reported that a 5% retention-rate increase can raise profits by 25% to 95%, while McKinsey cites next-best-experience programs with 5% to 8% revenue lift and 20% to 30% cost-to-serve reduction. [Bain Loyalty Rules](https://www.bain.com/contentassets/29f74ec417fa4e36a1d7d7e7479badc5/loyalty_rules_chapter_one.pdf); [McKinsey next-best-experience case examples](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/growth-marketing-and-sales/our-insights/next-best-experience-how-ai-can-power-every-customer-interaction)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **customer/account/session**. Decision: **present action/offer or suppress contact**. Label/outcome: **conversion, retention, satisfaction, cost-to-serve**. Horizon: **real time to next cycle**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: CRM, CDP, product analytics, billing/subscription, commerce, support, marketing automation, data warehouse. In practice this data often exists but needs cleaning; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to present action/offer or suppress contact; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are conversion, retention, satisfaction, cost-to-serve, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium-high: data usually exists but requires integration, standard definitions, and human-in-the-loop workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — SaaS defines churn at account or logo renewal; telecom and banking define it at subscriber/account level; retail often treats it as repeat purchase and spend decay; marketplaces treat it as merchant or buyer dormancy.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to personalization, offer optimization, customer service, pricing, and marketing attribution; the boundary is the business decision being changed, not the model feature set.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in scaled programs and advanced pilots: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: McKinsey reported a global payments processor could reduce merchant attrition by up to 20% per year, and Carrefour increased LTV ROAS of newly acquired customers by 21% using an LTV model. [McKinsey next-best-experience case examples](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/growth-marketing-and-sales/our-insights/next-best-experience-how-ai-can-power-every-customer-interaction); [Google/Carrefour LTV case study](https://services.google.com/fh/files/blogs/carrefour_case_study.pdf)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are unclear intervention economics, leakage from treatment into labels, uncoordinated channels, privacy/consent gaps, and optimizing short-term conversion while harming long-term value; BCG and MIT/BCG both show scaling value depends more on organizational learning and adoption capabilities than model availability alone. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Optimization: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.

### Personalization & product recommendations

1. **Name + aliases** — Personalization & product recommendations; aliases include recommendations, ranking, personalized merchandising, content personalization.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which products, content, bundles, or messages should be shown to improve conversion and relevance. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: customer. Outcome owner: chief revenue officer, head of growth, customer success, product, ecommerce, or pricing leader.
4. **Economic pool** — Retention and revenue-protection value is high: Bain reported that a 5% retention-rate increase can raise profits by 25% to 95%, while McKinsey cites next-best-experience programs with 5% to 8% revenue lift and 20% to 30% cost-to-serve reduction. [Bain Loyalty Rules](https://www.bain.com/contentassets/29f74ec417fa4e36a1d7d7e7479badc5/loyalty_rules_chapter_one.pdf); [McKinsey next-best-experience case examples](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/growth-marketing-and-sales/our-insights/next-best-experience-how-ai-can-power-every-customer-interaction)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **visitor/session/customer/item**. Decision: **rank and display products/content**. Label/outcome: **click, add-to-cart, purchase, engagement**. Horizon: **milliseconds to days**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: CRM, CDP, product analytics, billing/subscription, commerce, support, marketing automation, data warehouse. In practice this data commonly exists in usable form; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to rank and display products/content; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are click, add-to-cart, purchase, engagement, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — High: data and labels are usually captured in operational systems, and the action can be wired into existing workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — SaaS defines churn at account or logo renewal; telecom and banking define it at subscriber/account level; retail often treats it as repeat purchase and spend decay; marketplaces treat it as merchant or buyer dormancy.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to personalization, offer optimization, customer service, pricing, and marketing attribution; the boundary is the business decision being changed, not the model feature set.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is deployed at scale in many data-mature enterprises: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: McKinsey reported a global payments processor could reduce merchant attrition by up to 20% per year, and Carrefour increased LTV ROAS of newly acquired customers by 21% using an LTV model. [McKinsey next-best-experience case examples](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/growth-marketing-and-sales/our-insights/next-best-experience-how-ai-can-power-every-customer-interaction); [Google/Carrefour LTV case study](https://services.google.com/fh/files/blogs/carrefour_case_study.pdf)

### Lead scoring

1. **Name + aliases** — Lead scoring; aliases include propensity-to-buy, MQL scoring, fit scoring, qualification scoring.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which prospects are most likely to convert and deserve sales attention. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: customer. Outcome owner: chief revenue officer, head of growth, customer success, product, ecommerce, or pricing leader.
4. **Economic pool** — Retention and revenue-protection value is high: Bain reported that a 5% retention-rate increase can raise profits by 25% to 95%, while McKinsey cites next-best-experience programs with 5% to 8% revenue lift and 20% to 30% cost-to-serve reduction. [Bain Loyalty Rules](https://www.bain.com/contentassets/29f74ec417fa4e36a1d7d7e7479badc5/loyalty_rules_chapter_one.pdf); [McKinsey next-best-experience case examples](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/growth-marketing-and-sales/our-insights/next-best-experience-how-ai-can-power-every-customer-interaction)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **lead/account/contact**. Decision: **route, prioritize, nurture, or disqualify**. Label/outcome: **qualified opportunity or sale**. Horizon: **days to quarters**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates in large portfolios, shared services, or high-cost workflows.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: CRM, CDP, product analytics, billing/subscription, commerce, support, marketing automation, data warehouse. In practice this data often exists but needs cleaning; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to route, prioritize, nurture, or disqualify; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are qualified opportunity or sale, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium-high: data usually exists but requires integration, standard definitions, and human-in-the-loop workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — SaaS defines churn at account or logo renewal; telecom and banking define it at subscriber/account level; retail often treats it as repeat purchase and spend decay; marketplaces treat it as merchant or buyer dormancy.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to personalization, offer optimization, customer service, pricing, and marketing attribution; the boundary is the business decision being changed, not the model feature set.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in scaled programs and advanced pilots: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: McKinsey reported a global payments processor could reduce merchant attrition by up to 20% per year, and Carrefour increased LTV ROAS of newly acquired customers by 21% using an LTV model. [McKinsey next-best-experience case examples](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/growth-marketing-and-sales/our-insights/next-best-experience-how-ai-can-power-every-customer-interaction); [Google/Carrefour LTV case study](https://services.google.com/fh/files/blogs/carrefour_case_study.pdf)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are unclear intervention economics, leakage from treatment into labels, uncoordinated channels, privacy/consent gaps, and optimizing short-term conversion while harming long-term value; BCG and MIT/BCG both show scaling value depends more on organizational learning and adoption capabilities than model availability alone. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Scoring: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [Bain Loyalty Rules](https://www.bain.com/contentassets/29f74ec417fa4e36a1d7d7e7479badc5/loyalty_rules_chapter_one.pdf); [McKinsey next-best-experience case examples](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/growth-marketing-and-sales/our-insights/next-best-experience-how-ai-can-power-every-customer-interaction); [Google/Carrefour LTV case study](https://services.google.com/fh/files/blogs/carrefour_case_study.pdf); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Opportunity / win prediction

1. **Name + aliases** — Opportunity / win prediction; aliases include deal scoring, forecast category risk, pipeline health.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which open deals are likely to close, slip, shrink, or lose. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: sales. Outcome owner: CRO, VP Sales, revenue operations, finance forecasting leader.
4. **Economic pool** — Sales-forecast and pipeline errors concentrate value because 80% of surveyed sales and finance teams missed at least one quarterly forecast in the prior year, and over half missed two or more. [Xactly 2024 Sales Forecasting Benchmark Report](https://www.xactlycorp.com/sites/default/files/file/2024-07/2024_sales_forecasting_benchmark_report.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **opportunity/deal**. Decision: **coach, inspect, discount, advance, or de-prioritize**. Label/outcome: **closed-won amount and timing**. Horizon: **current quarter to 12 months**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from thousands to hundreds of thousands of daily or weekly decisions; value concentrates in large portfolios, shared services, or high-cost workflows.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: CRM, CPQ, sales-engagement, billing, ERP, territory/quota systems, data warehouse. In practice this data often exists but needs cleaning; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to coach, inspect, discount, advance, or de-prioritize; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are closed-won amount and timing, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium-high: data usually exists but requires integration, standard definitions, and human-in-the-loop workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Executive analytics cuts across functions; the material variant is whether it is used for real operating decisions or only static reporting.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to every function; cross-functional use cases synthesize decision evidence rather than own frontline execution.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in scaled programs and advanced pilots: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: BCG found only 26% of companies had moved beyond proofs of concept to tangible value in 2024, and future-built companies in 2025 achieved five times the revenue increases and three times the cost reductions of others. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [BCG 2025 AI value-gap study](https://www.bcg.com/publications/2025/are-you-generating-value-from-ai-the-widening-gap)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are dashboards without owners, metric definitions that differ by function, hallucinated narrative, lack of drill-through, and executive decisions not tied to operating actions. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Scoring: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [Xactly 2024 Sales Forecasting Benchmark Report](https://www.xactlycorp.com/sites/default/files/file/2024-07/2024_sales_forecasting_benchmark_report.pdf); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Sales forecasting

1. **Name + aliases** — Sales forecasting; aliases include revenue forecasting, bookings forecast, pipeline forecast.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is how much revenue, bookings, ARR, or volume will close by period and segment. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: sales. Outcome owner: CRO, VP Sales, revenue operations, finance forecasting leader.
4. **Economic pool** — Sales-forecast and pipeline errors concentrate value because 80% of surveyed sales and finance teams missed at least one quarterly forecast in the prior year, and over half missed two or more. [Xactly 2024 Sales Forecasting Benchmark Report](https://www.xactlycorp.com/sites/default/files/file/2024-07/2024_sales_forecasting_benchmark_report.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **territory/product/rep/opportunity aggregate**. Decision: **commit forecast, capacity, hiring, spend**. Label/outcome: **actual sales vs forecast**. Horizon: **weekly to quarterly**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from thousands to hundreds of thousands of daily or weekly decisions; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: CRM, CPQ, sales-engagement, billing, ERP, territory/quota systems, data warehouse. In practice this data often exists but needs cleaning; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to commit forecast, capacity, hiring, spend; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are actual sales vs forecast, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium-high: data usually exists but requires integration, standard definitions, and human-in-the-loop workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Executive analytics cuts across functions; the material variant is whether it is used for real operating decisions or only static reporting.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to every function; cross-functional use cases synthesize decision evidence rather than own frontline execution.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in scaled programs and advanced pilots: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: BCG found only 26% of companies had moved beyond proofs of concept to tangible value in 2024, and future-built companies in 2025 achieved five times the revenue increases and three times the cost reductions of others. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [BCG 2025 AI value-gap study](https://www.bcg.com/publications/2025/are-you-generating-value-from-ai-the-widening-gap)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are dashboards without owners, metric definitions that differ by function, hallucinated narrative, lack of drill-through, and executive decisions not tied to operating actions. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Forecasting: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [Xactly 2024 Sales Forecasting Benchmark Report](https://www.xactlycorp.com/sites/default/files/file/2024-07/2024_sales_forecasting_benchmark_report.pdf); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Pricing & discount optimization

1. **Name + aliases** — Pricing & discount optimization; aliases include dynamic pricing, markdown, price guidance, deal desk optimization.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is what price or discount should be offered to maximize revenue, margin, win rate, and fairness. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: customer. Outcome owner: chief revenue officer, head of growth, customer success, product, ecommerce, or pricing leader.
4. **Economic pool** — Retention and revenue-protection value is high: Bain reported that a 5% retention-rate increase can raise profits by 25% to 95%, while McKinsey cites next-best-experience programs with 5% to 8% revenue lift and 20% to 30% cost-to-serve reduction. [Bain Loyalty Rules](https://www.bain.com/contentassets/29f74ec417fa4e36a1d7d7e7479badc5/loyalty_rules_chapter_one.pdf); [McKinsey next-best-experience case examples](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/growth-marketing-and-sales/our-insights/next-best-experience-how-ai-can-power-every-customer-interaction)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **SKU/offer/deal/customer**. Decision: **set price, discount, guardrail, or approval**. Label/outcome: **margin, conversion, win rate**. Horizon: **real time to season**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from thousands to hundreds of thousands of daily or weekly decisions; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: CRM, CDP, product analytics, billing/subscription, commerce, support, marketing automation, data warehouse. In practice this data exists unevenly and usually needs standardization; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to set price, discount, guardrail, or approval; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are margin, conversion, win rate, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium: labels, causality, process standardization, or integration are often the bottleneck. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — SaaS defines churn at account or logo renewal; telecom and banking define it at subscriber/account level; retail often treats it as repeat purchase and spend decay; marketplaces treat it as merchant or buyer dormancy.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to personalization, offer optimization, customer service, pricing, and marketing attribution; the boundary is the business decision being changed, not the model feature set.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in pilots but less consistently scaled: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: McKinsey reported a global payments processor could reduce merchant attrition by up to 20% per year, and Carrefour increased LTV ROAS of newly acquired customers by 21% using an LTV model. [McKinsey next-best-experience case examples](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/growth-marketing-and-sales/our-insights/next-best-experience-how-ai-can-power-every-customer-interaction); [Google/Carrefour LTV case study](https://services.google.com/fh/files/blogs/carrefour_case_study.pdf)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are unclear intervention economics, leakage from treatment into labels, uncoordinated channels, privacy/consent gaps, and optimizing short-term conversion while harming long-term value; BCG and MIT/BCG both show scaling value depends more on organizational learning and adoption capabilities than model availability alone. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Optimization: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [Bain Loyalty Rules](https://www.bain.com/contentassets/29f74ec417fa4e36a1d7d7e7479badc5/loyalty_rules_chapter_one.pdf); [McKinsey next-best-experience case examples](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/growth-marketing-and-sales/our-insights/next-best-experience-how-ai-can-power-every-customer-interaction); [Google/Carrefour LTV case study](https://services.google.com/fh/files/blogs/carrefour_case_study.pdf); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Promotion effectiveness

1. **Name + aliases** — Promotion effectiveness; aliases include promo lift, trade promotion optimization, campaign incrementality.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which promotions create incremental sales rather than subsidizing purchases that would happen anyway. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: marketing. Outcome owner: CMO, growth marketing, lifecycle marketing, performance marketing, brand/content leader.
4. **Economic pool** — Marketing optimization matters because the Fall 2024 CMO Survey placed marketing budgets at 7.7% of company revenue and projected 12.7% digital-marketing-spend growth over the next 12 months. [The CMO Survey Fall 2024](https://cmosurvey.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/11/The_CMO_Survey-Highlights_and_Insights_Report-Fall_2024.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **promotion/customer/SKU/store**. Decision: **fund, change, stop, or reallocate promotion**. Label/outcome: **incremental margin/lift**. Horizon: **campaign to season**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from hundreds to thousands of recurring decisions; value concentrates in large portfolios, shared services, or high-cost workflows.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: CDP, DMP, ad platforms, web analytics, email/SMS platforms, CRM, consent management, data clean rooms. In practice this data exists unevenly and usually needs standardization; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to fund, change, stop, or reallocate promotion; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are incremental margin/lift, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium: labels, causality, process standardization, or integration are often the bottleneck. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — B2C variants optimize impressions, channels, and products; B2B variants optimize accounts, buying committees, and pipeline; regulated industries add consent and fairness constraints.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to sales and personalization; marketing owns spend allocation, audience/contact policy, and message/creative performance.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in pilots but less consistently scaled: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: Carrefour reported +21% LTV ROAS and a target audience with 3x more annual purchases and 25% higher cart revenue; McKinsey reported next-best-experience programs can raise revenue 5% to 8%. [Google/Carrefour LTV case study](https://services.google.com/fh/files/blogs/carrefour_case_study.pdf); [McKinsey next-best-experience case examples](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/growth-marketing-and-sales/our-insights/next-best-experience-how-ai-can-power-every-customer-interaction)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are selection bias, attribution confounding, channel silos, creative fatigue, bad incrementality measurement, and consent limitations; experiments or geo-holdouts are needed because platform-reported ROAS often overstates causal lift. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Causal: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [The CMO Survey Fall 2024](https://cmosurvey.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/11/The_CMO_Survey-Highlights_and_Insights_Report-Fall_2024.pdf); [Google/Carrefour LTV case study](https://services.google.com/fh/files/blogs/carrefour_case_study.pdf); [McKinsey next-best-experience case examples](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/growth-marketing-and-sales/our-insights/next-best-experience-how-ai-can-power-every-customer-interaction); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Market-basket / cross-sell

1. **Name + aliases** — Market-basket / cross-sell; aliases include affinity analysis, attach prediction, basket expansion.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which products or services should be bundled or suggested together. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: customer. Outcome owner: chief revenue officer, head of growth, customer success, product, ecommerce, or pricing leader.
4. **Economic pool** — Retention and revenue-protection value is high: Bain reported that a 5% retention-rate increase can raise profits by 25% to 95%, while McKinsey cites next-best-experience programs with 5% to 8% revenue lift and 20% to 30% cost-to-serve reduction. [Bain Loyalty Rules](https://www.bain.com/contentassets/29f74ec417fa4e36a1d7d7e7479badc5/loyalty_rules_chapter_one.pdf); [McKinsey next-best-experience case examples](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/growth-marketing-and-sales/our-insights/next-best-experience-how-ai-can-power-every-customer-interaction)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **customer/basket/SKU/account**. Decision: **recommend, bundle, sequence, or prompt seller**. Label/outcome: **attach, basket value, penetration**. Horizon: **session to account plan**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates in large portfolios, shared services, or high-cost workflows.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: CRM, CDP, product analytics, billing/subscription, commerce, support, marketing automation, data warehouse. In practice this data commonly exists in usable form; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to recommend, bundle, sequence, or prompt seller; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are attach, basket value, penetration, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — High: data and labels are usually captured in operational systems, and the action can be wired into existing workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — SaaS defines churn at account or logo renewal; telecom and banking define it at subscriber/account level; retail often treats it as repeat purchase and spend decay; marketplaces treat it as merchant or buyer dormancy.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to personalization, offer optimization, customer service, pricing, and marketing attribution; the boundary is the business decision being changed, not the model feature set.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is deployed at scale in many data-mature enterprises: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: McKinsey reported a global payments processor could reduce merchant attrition by up to 20% per year, and Carrefour increased LTV ROAS of newly acquired customers by 21% using an LTV model. [McKinsey next-best-experience case examples](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/growth-marketing-and-sales/our-insights/next-best-experience-how-ai-can-power-every-customer-interaction); [Google/Carrefour LTV case study](https://services.google.com/fh/files/blogs/carrefour_case_study.pdf)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are unclear intervention economics, leakage from treatment into labels, uncoordinated channels, privacy/consent gaps, and optimizing short-term conversion while harming long-term value; BCG and MIT/BCG both show scaling value depends more on organizational learning and adoption capabilities than model availability alone. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Association: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [Bain Loyalty Rules](https://www.bain.com/contentassets/29f74ec417fa4e36a1d7d7e7479badc5/loyalty_rules_chapter_one.pdf); [McKinsey next-best-experience case examples](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/growth-marketing-and-sales/our-insights/next-best-experience-how-ai-can-power-every-customer-interaction); [Google/Carrefour LTV case study](https://services.google.com/fh/files/blogs/carrefour_case_study.pdf); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Customer feedback & sentiment analysis

1. **Name + aliases** — Customer feedback & sentiment analysis; aliases include sentiment, review mining, survey analytics.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is what customers are saying and which issues are driving satisfaction, churn, and brand perception. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: support. Outcome owner: chief customer officer, VP support, contact-center leader, CX operations, knowledge leader.
4. **Economic pool** — Labor is the largest support cost: contact-center frontline labor and supervision commonly account for roughly 67% to 76% of operating cost, so deflection, routing, and handle-time reduction have direct labor leverage. [Strategic Contact contact-center cost study](https://www.strategiccontact.com/pdf/CC_Cost_WP.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **review/ticket/call/social post**. Decision: **route issue, fix product, message recovery**. Label/outcome: **sentiment, CSAT, issue volume**. Horizon: **daily to quarterly**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates where senior decision latency or scarce expertise matters.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: CRM/case management, contact-center platform, telephony, chat, ticketing, knowledge base, product telemetry, QA tools. In practice this data commonly exists in usable form; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to route issue, fix product, message recovery; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are sentiment, CSAT, issue volume, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — High: data and labels are usually captured in operational systems, and the action can be wired into existing workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — B2C support is high-volume and real-time; B2B support is lower-volume but higher-value and SLA-driven; regulated support adds auditability and compliance constraints.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to VoC, product analytics, knowledge management, and churn; support use cases own the service workflow and customer-contact outcome.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is deployed at scale in many data-mature enterprises: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: Klarna’s AI assistant handled 2.3 million conversations, two-thirds of chats, with a 25% drop in repeat inquiries and estimated $40 million 2024 profit improvement; Vodafone reported 99% faster journey-testing turnaround for TOBi. [Klarna AI assistant 2024 announcement](https://www.klarna.com/international/press/klarna-ai-assistant-handles-two-thirds-of-customer-service-chats-in-its-first-month/); [IBM Vodafone TOBi case study](https://www.ibm.com/case-studies/vodafone-tobi)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are hallucinated or outdated answers, weak knowledge governance, missing CRM/ITSM writeback, poor escalation design, and measuring deflection instead of solved issues; NIST stresses mapping, measuring, and managing AI risks across lifecycle. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — NLP: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [Strategic Contact contact-center cost study](https://www.strategiccontact.com/pdf/CC_Cost_WP.pdf); [Klarna AI assistant 2024 announcement](https://www.klarna.com/international/press/klarna-ai-assistant-handles-two-thirds-of-customer-service-chats-in-its-first-month/); [IBM Vodafone TOBi case study](https://www.ibm.com/case-studies/vodafone-tobi); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Voice-of-customer mining

1. **Name + aliases** — Voice-of-customer mining; aliases include VoC mining, experience analytics, theme detection.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which recurring unmet needs and friction points appear across calls, surveys, chats, reviews, and communities. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: support. Outcome owner: chief customer officer, VP support, contact-center leader, CX operations, knowledge leader.
4. **Economic pool** — Labor is the largest support cost: contact-center frontline labor and supervision commonly account for roughly 67% to 76% of operating cost, so deflection, routing, and handle-time reduction have direct labor leverage. [Strategic Contact contact-center cost study](https://www.strategiccontact.com/pdf/CC_Cost_WP.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **utterance/document/theme/customer journey**. Decision: **open defect, create KB, change policy, prioritize roadmap**. Label/outcome: **NPS drivers, complaints, issue recurrence**. Horizon: **weekly to monthly**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from thousands to hundreds of thousands of daily or weekly decisions; value concentrates where senior decision latency or scarce expertise matters.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: CRM/case management, contact-center platform, telephony, chat, ticketing, knowledge base, product telemetry, QA tools. In practice this data often exists but needs cleaning; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to open defect, create KB, change policy, prioritize roadmap; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are NPS drivers, complaints, issue recurrence, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium-high: data usually exists but requires integration, standard definitions, and human-in-the-loop workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — B2C support is high-volume and real-time; B2B support is lower-volume but higher-value and SLA-driven; regulated support adds auditability and compliance constraints.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to VoC, product analytics, knowledge management, and churn; support use cases own the service workflow and customer-contact outcome.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in scaled programs and advanced pilots: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: Klarna’s AI assistant handled 2.3 million conversations, two-thirds of chats, with a 25% drop in repeat inquiries and estimated $40 million 2024 profit improvement; Vodafone reported 99% faster journey-testing turnaround for TOBi. [Klarna AI assistant 2024 announcement](https://www.klarna.com/international/press/klarna-ai-assistant-handles-two-thirds-of-customer-service-chats-in-its-first-month/); [IBM Vodafone TOBi case study](https://www.ibm.com/case-studies/vodafone-tobi)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are hallucinated or outdated answers, weak knowledge governance, missing CRM/ITSM writeback, poor escalation design, and measuring deflection instead of solved issues; NIST stresses mapping, measuring, and managing AI risks across lifecycle. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)

## B. Customer service & support

### Ticket triage & routing

1. **Name + aliases** — Ticket triage & routing; aliases include case routing, skill-based routing, queue prioritization.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which support request should go to which queue, agent, SLA, or workflow. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: support. Outcome owner: chief customer officer, VP support, contact-center leader, CX operations, knowledge leader.
4. **Economic pool** — Labor is the largest support cost: contact-center frontline labor and supervision commonly account for roughly 67% to 76% of operating cost, so deflection, routing, and handle-time reduction have direct labor leverage. [Strategic Contact contact-center cost study](https://www.strategiccontact.com/pdf/CC_Cost_WP.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **ticket/case/message**. Decision: **route, prioritize, escalate, merge**. Label/outcome: **first-contact resolution, SLA, reassignment**. Horizon: **real time**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates in large portfolios, shared services, or high-cost workflows.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: CRM/case management, contact-center platform, telephony, chat, ticketing, knowledge base, product telemetry, QA tools. In practice this data commonly exists in usable form; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to route, prioritize, escalate, merge; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are first-contact resolution, SLA, reassignment, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — High: data and labels are usually captured in operational systems, and the action can be wired into existing workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [Klarna AI assistant 2024 announcement](https://www.klarna.com/international/press/klarna-ai-assistant-handles-two-thirds-of-customer-service-chats-in-its-first-month/); [IBM Vodafone TOBi case study](https://www.ibm.com/case-studies/vodafone-tobi)

### Response drafting / resolution automation

2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is how to answer a customer accurately and safely with minimal manual effort. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: support. Outcome owner: chief customer officer, VP support, contact-center leader, CX operations, knowledge leader.

6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
