## E. Fraud, risk & compliance

### Transaction fraud

1. **Name + aliases** — Transaction fraud; aliases include payment fraud, card-not-present fraud, fraud scoring.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which transaction is fraudulent or too risky to approve. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: risk. Outcome owner: chief risk officer, fraud operations, trust and safety, payments risk, investigations.
4. **Economic pool** — Fraud is a direct loss pool: ACFE estimated organizations lose 5% of revenue to fraud annually, and CyberSource reported merchants estimate 3% of ecommerce revenue is lost to fraud each year. [ACFE 2024 Report to the Nations](https://www.acfe.com/-/media/files/acfe/pdfs/rttn/2024/2024-report-to-the-nations.pdf); [2024 Global eCommerce Payments & Fraud Report](https://www.cybersource.com/content/dam/documents/campaign/fraud-report/global-fraud-report-2024.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **transaction/order/payment**. Decision: **approve, decline, step-up, hold, investigate**. Label/outcome: **fraud loss, false positives, chargebacks**. Horizon: **milliseconds**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: payment gateway, order management, device/session telemetry, chargeback systems, case management, identity/KYC, graph store. In practice this data commonly exists in usable form; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to approve, decline, step-up, hold, investigate; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are fraud loss, false positives, chargebacks, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — High: data and labels are usually captured in operational systems, and the action can be wired into existing workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Payments emphasize millisecond decisions and false-positive cost; insurance and public-sector fraud emphasize investigation yield; internal fraud emphasizes audit evidence and controls.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to cybersecurity and compliance; fraud use cases decide monetary approval, investigation, or recovery, whereas cyber use cases decide technical containment.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is deployed at scale in many data-mature enterprises: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: Mastercard reported average fraud-detection-rate improvement of 20%, up to 300% in some cases, and false-positive reduction of more than 85%; CyberSource reported ecommerce merchants use one to two AI/ML fraud tools on average. [Mastercard Decision Intelligence Pro 2024 announcement](https://www.mastercard.com/us/en/news-and-trends/press/2024/february/mastercard-supercharges-consumer-protection-with-gen-ai.html); [2024 Global eCommerce Payments & Fraud Report](https://www.cybersource.com/content/dam/documents/campaign/fraud-report/global-fraud-report-2024.pdf)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are adversarial adaptation, false positives that block good customers, delayed labels, investigation bottlenecks, and weak feedback from chargebacks or SAR outcomes. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Scoring: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [ACFE 2024 Report to the Nations](https://www.acfe.com/-/media/files/acfe/pdfs/rttn/2024/2024-report-to-the-nations.pdf); [2024 Global eCommerce Payments & Fraud Report](https://www.cybersource.com/content/dam/documents/campaign/fraud-report/global-fraud-report-2024.pdf); [Mastercard Decision Intelligence Pro 2024 announcement](https://www.mastercard.com/us/en/news-and-trends/press/2024/february/mastercard-supercharges-consumer-protection-with-gen-ai.html); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Account takeover

1. **Name + aliases** — Account takeover; aliases include ATO, credential compromise, session risk.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which login, session, or account change indicates account compromise. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: risk. Outcome owner: chief risk officer, fraud operations, trust and safety, payments risk, investigations.
4. **Economic pool** — Fraud is a direct loss pool: ACFE estimated organizations lose 5% of revenue to fraud annually, and CyberSource reported merchants estimate 3% of ecommerce revenue is lost to fraud each year. [ACFE 2024 Report to the Nations](https://www.acfe.com/-/media/files/acfe/pdfs/rttn/2024/2024-report-to-the-nations.pdf); [2024 Global eCommerce Payments & Fraud Report](https://www.cybersource.com/content/dam/documents/campaign/fraud-report/global-fraud-report-2024.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **login/session/account event**. Decision: **step-up, lock, notify, investigate**. Label/outcome: **ATO loss, takeover rate, friction**. Horizon: **real time**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: payment gateway, order management, device/session telemetry, chargeback systems, case management, identity/KYC, graph store. In practice this data often exists but needs cleaning; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to step-up, lock, notify, investigate; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are ATO loss, takeover rate, friction, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium-high: data usually exists but requires integration, standard definitions, and human-in-the-loop workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Payments emphasize millisecond decisions and false-positive cost; insurance and public-sector fraud emphasize investigation yield; internal fraud emphasizes audit evidence and controls.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to cybersecurity and compliance; fraud use cases decide monetary approval, investigation, or recovery, whereas cyber use cases decide technical containment.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in scaled programs and advanced pilots: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: Mastercard reported average fraud-detection-rate improvement of 20%, up to 300% in some cases, and false-positive reduction of more than 85%; CyberSource reported ecommerce merchants use one to two AI/ML fraud tools on average. [Mastercard Decision Intelligence Pro 2024 announcement](https://www.mastercard.com/us/en/news-and-trends/press/2024/february/mastercard-supercharges-consumer-protection-with-gen-ai.html); [2024 Global eCommerce Payments & Fraud Report](https://www.cybersource.com/content/dam/documents/campaign/fraud-report/global-fraud-report-2024.pdf)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are adversarial adaptation, false positives that block good customers, delayed labels, investigation bottlenecks, and weak feedback from chargebacks or SAR outcomes. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Anomaly: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [ACFE 2024 Report to the Nations](https://www.acfe.com/-/media/files/acfe/pdfs/rttn/2024/2024-report-to-the-nations.pdf); [2024 Global eCommerce Payments & Fraud Report](https://www.cybersource.com/content/dam/documents/campaign/fraud-report/global-fraud-report-2024.pdf); [Mastercard Decision Intelligence Pro 2024 announcement](https://www.mastercard.com/us/en/news-and-trends/press/2024/february/mastercard-supercharges-consumer-protection-with-gen-ai.html); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### AML / financial-crime detection

1. **Name + aliases** — AML / financial-crime detection; aliases include AML alerting, suspicious activity monitoring, transaction monitoring.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which customer activity may constitute money laundering, terrorist financing, or other reportable financial crime. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: risk. Outcome owner: chief compliance officer, BSA/AML officer, financial-crime operations, KYC operations.
4. **Economic pool** — Financial-crime compliance is a major cost pool: LexisNexis estimated global financial-crime compliance costs for financial institutions at US$206.1 billion in 2023. [LexisNexis 2023 financial-crime compliance study](https://risk.lexisnexis.com/about-us/press-room/press-release/20230926-global-financial-crime-compliance-costs)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **customer/transaction/network**. Decision: **alert, investigate, SAR, exit relationship**. Label/outcome: **SAR quality, false positives, risk exposure**. Horizon: **daily/monthly**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from thousands to hundreds of thousands of daily or weekly decisions; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: core banking, transaction monitoring, KYC/CDD, sanctions lists, adverse-media feeds, case management, regulatory reporting. In practice this data exists unevenly and usually needs standardization; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to alert, investigate, SAR, exit relationship; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are SAR quality, false positives, risk exposure, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium: labels, causality, process standardization, or integration are often the bottleneck. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Banking and fintech variants are heavily regulated and alert-investigation driven; nonfinancial firms mainly use sanctions, adverse media, and third-party screening.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to fraud and KYC; AML is about reportable financial crime and regulatory obligations rather than immediate transaction loss alone.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in pilots but less consistently scaled: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: BCG found only 26% of companies had moved beyond proofs of concept to tangible value in 2024, and future-built companies in 2025 achieved five times the revenue increases and three times the cost reductions of others. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [BCG 2025 AI value-gap study](https://www.bcg.com/publications/2025/are-you-generating-value-from-ai-the-widening-gap)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are high false positives, poor entity resolution, siloed customer data, regulatory conservatism, and inability to explain alerts to investigators and examiners. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Graph: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [LexisNexis 2023 financial-crime compliance study](https://risk.lexisnexis.com/about-us/press-room/press-release/20230926-global-financial-crime-compliance-costs); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### KYC screening

1. **Name + aliases** — KYC screening; aliases include CDD, onboarding risk, identity verification.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is whether a customer, beneficial owner, or entity can be onboarded or maintained safely. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: risk. Outcome owner: chief compliance officer, BSA/AML officer, financial-crime operations, KYC operations.
4. **Economic pool** — Financial-crime compliance is a major cost pool: LexisNexis estimated global financial-crime compliance costs for financial institutions at US$206.1 billion in 2023. [LexisNexis 2023 financial-crime compliance study](https://risk.lexisnexis.com/about-us/press-room/press-release/20230926-global-financial-crime-compliance-costs)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **person/entity/application**. Decision: **approve, request evidence, reject, monitor**. Label/outcome: **onboarding time, risk flags, compliance findings**. Horizon: **real time/daily**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from thousands to hundreds of thousands of daily or weekly decisions; value concentrates in large portfolios, shared services, or high-cost workflows.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: core banking, transaction monitoring, KYC/CDD, sanctions lists, adverse-media feeds, case management, regulatory reporting. In practice this data often exists but needs cleaning; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to approve, request evidence, reject, monitor; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are onboarding time, risk flags, compliance findings, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium-high: data usually exists but requires integration, standard definitions, and human-in-the-loop workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Banking and fintech variants are heavily regulated and alert-investigation driven; nonfinancial firms mainly use sanctions, adverse media, and third-party screening.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to fraud and KYC; AML is about reportable financial crime and regulatory obligations rather than immediate transaction loss alone.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in scaled programs and advanced pilots: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: BCG found only 26% of companies had moved beyond proofs of concept to tangible value in 2024, and future-built companies in 2025 achieved five times the revenue increases and three times the cost reductions of others. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [BCG 2025 AI value-gap study](https://www.bcg.com/publications/2025/are-you-generating-value-from-ai-the-widening-gap)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are high false positives, poor entity resolution, siloed customer data, regulatory conservatism, and inability to explain alerts to investigators and examiners. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Classification: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [LexisNexis 2023 financial-crime compliance study](https://risk.lexisnexis.com/about-us/press-room/press-release/20230926-global-financial-crime-compliance-costs); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Underwriting risk

1. **Name + aliases** — Underwriting risk; aliases include risk selection, pricing risk, loss prediction.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which applicant, policy, loan, or exposure will generate adverse losses relative to price. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: risk. Outcome owner: CFO, controller, treasurer, FP&A, credit, AP/AR, or shared-services leader.
4. **Economic pool** — Finance automation value comes from working capital, payment risk, and labor: PwC estimated €1.56 trillion of global excess working capital in 2024/25, and AFP found 79% of organizations experienced actual or attempted payments fraud in 2024. [PwC Working Capital Study 2024/25](https://www.pwc.co.uk/services/value-creation/insights/working-capital-study/2024.html); [2025 AFP Payments Fraud and Control Survey](https://www.truist.com/content/dam/truist-bank/us/en/documents/info/cci/2025-afp-payments-fraud-control-survey-report-key-highlights.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **application/policy/borrower/exposure**. Decision: **approve, price, limit, reinsure, decline**. Label/outcome: **loss ratio, default rate, margin**. Horizon: **real time to renewal**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from thousands to hundreds of thousands of daily or weekly decisions; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: ERP, AP/AR, treasury, TMS, bank statements, expense management, GL, close/reconciliation tools, procurement systems. In practice this data often exists but needs cleaning; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to approve, price, limit, reinsure, decline; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are loss ratio, default rate, margin, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium-high: data usually exists but requires integration, standard definitions, and human-in-the-loop workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Large enterprises have ERP/AP/AR history; banks and insurers add regulated model governance; smaller firms often lack clean labels and standardized workflows.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to procurement, risk, and operations; finance use cases own cash, controls, close, and financial planning outcomes.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in scaled programs and advanced pilots: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: Genpact reported an enterprise AP program reaching 80% touchless processing, while HighRadius reported Staples reduced bad debt by 20% using collections automation. [Genpact 80% touchless AP case study](https://www.genpact.com/case-studies/from-manual-to-80-percent-touchless-accounts-payable-at-enterprise-scale); [HighRadius Staples collections case study](https://www.highradius.com/resources/Radiance/20-reduction-in-bad-debt-staples-collections-automation-journey-with-ai/)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are inconsistent master data, unclear approval authority, exception-heavy processes, audit concerns, and automating broken controls; payment-fraud prevalence and working-capital scale make false positives and control design material. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Scoring: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [PwC Working Capital Study 2024/25](https://www.pwc.co.uk/services/value-creation/insights/working-capital-study/2024.html); [2025 AFP Payments Fraud and Control Survey](https://www.truist.com/content/dam/truist-bank/us/en/documents/info/cci/2025-afp-payments-fraud-control-survey-report-key-highlights.pdf); [Genpact 80% touchless AP case study](https://www.genpact.com/case-studies/from-manual-to-80-percent-touchless-accounts-payable-at-enterprise-scale); [HighRadius Staples collections case study](https://www.highradius.com/resources/Radiance/20-reduction-in-bad-debt-staples-collections-automation-journey-with-ai/); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Model risk & regulatory reporting

1. **Name + aliases** — Model risk & regulatory reporting; aliases include model monitoring, SR 11-7 support, AI governance reporting.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which models are drifting, noncompliant, unfair, or insufficiently documented. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: risk. Outcome owner: CEO, COO, CFO, strategy, transformation office, corporate development, chief of staff.
4. **Economic pool** — Cross-functional analytics value is the management operating system: McKinsey estimated genAI could add $2.6 trillion to $4.4 trillion annually across 63 use cases, with roughly 75% of value in customer operations, marketing and sales, software engineering, and R&D. [McKinsey 2023 generative AI economic-potential report](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/tech-and-ai/our-insights/the-economic-potential-of-generative-ai-the-next-productivity-frontier)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **model/report/control**. Decision: **validate, remediate, restrict, document**. Label/outcome: **findings, drift, audit closure**. Horizon: **monthly/quarterly**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from hundreds to thousands of recurring decisions; value concentrates where senior decision latency or scarce expertise matters.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: data warehouse/lakehouse, BI, planning, ERP, CRM, HRIS, external market/news data, board-reporting systems. In practice this data exists unevenly and usually needs standardization; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to validate, remediate, restrict, document; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are findings, drift, audit closure, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium: labels, causality, process standardization, or integration are often the bottleneck. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Executive analytics cuts across functions; the material variant is whether it is used for real operating decisions or only static reporting.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to every function; cross-functional use cases synthesize decision evidence rather than own frontline execution.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in pilots but less consistently scaled: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: BCG found only 26% of companies had moved beyond proofs of concept to tangible value in 2024, and future-built companies in 2025 achieved five times the revenue increases and three times the cost reductions of others. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [BCG 2025 AI value-gap study](https://www.bcg.com/publications/2025/are-you-generating-value-from-ai-the-widening-gap)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are dashboards without owners, metric definitions that differ by function, hallucinated narrative, lack of drill-through, and executive decisions not tied to operating actions. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Monitoring: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [McKinsey 2023 generative AI economic-potential report](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/tech-and-ai/our-insights/the-economic-potential-of-generative-ai-the-next-productivity-frontier); [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [BCG 2025 AI value-gap study](https://www.bcg.com/publications/2025/are-you-generating-value-from-ai-the-widening-gap); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Sanctions / adverse-media screening

1. **Name + aliases** — Sanctions / adverse-media screening; aliases include watchlist screening, PEP screening, negative-news screening.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is whether a person or entity matches a restricted party or material risk signal. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: risk. Outcome owner: chief compliance officer, BSA/AML officer, financial-crime operations, KYC operations.
4. **Economic pool** — Financial-crime compliance is a major cost pool: LexisNexis estimated global financial-crime compliance costs for financial institutions at US$206.1 billion in 2023. [LexisNexis 2023 financial-crime compliance study](https://risk.lexisnexis.com/about-us/press-room/press-release/20230926-global-financial-crime-compliance-costs)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **entity/name/document/news item**. Decision: **clear, escalate, block, report**. Label/outcome: **false positives, sanctions misses**. Horizon: **real time/daily**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from thousands to hundreds of thousands of daily or weekly decisions; value concentrates in large portfolios, shared services, or high-cost workflows.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: core banking, transaction monitoring, KYC/CDD, sanctions lists, adverse-media feeds, case management, regulatory reporting. In practice this data often exists but needs cleaning; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to clear, escalate, block, report; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are false positives, sanctions misses, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium-high: data usually exists but requires integration, standard definitions, and human-in-the-loop workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Banking and fintech variants are heavily regulated and alert-investigation driven; nonfinancial firms mainly use sanctions, adverse media, and third-party screening.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to fraud and KYC; AML is about reportable financial crime and regulatory obligations rather than immediate transaction loss alone.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in scaled programs and advanced pilots: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: BCG found only 26% of companies had moved beyond proofs of concept to tangible value in 2024, and future-built companies in 2025 achieved five times the revenue increases and three times the cost reductions of others. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [BCG 2025 AI value-gap study](https://www.bcg.com/publications/2025/are-you-generating-value-from-ai-the-widening-gap)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are high false positives, poor entity resolution, siloed customer data, regulatory conservatism, and inability to explain alerts to investigators and examiners. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Entity: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [LexisNexis 2023 financial-crime compliance study](https://risk.lexisnexis.com/about-us/press-room/press-release/20230926-global-financial-crime-compliance-costs); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Policy compliance monitoring

1. **Name + aliases** — Policy compliance monitoring; aliases include communications surveillance, conduct risk, policy breach detection.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which employee, transaction, document, or conversation violates policy. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: risk. Outcome owner: chief risk officer, fraud operations, trust and safety, payments risk, investigations.
4. **Economic pool** — Fraud is a direct loss pool: ACFE estimated organizations lose 5% of revenue to fraud annually, and CyberSource reported merchants estimate 3% of ecommerce revenue is lost to fraud each year. [ACFE 2024 Report to the Nations](https://www.acfe.com/-/media/files/acfe/pdfs/rttn/2024/2024-report-to-the-nations.pdf); [2024 Global eCommerce Payments & Fraud Report](https://www.cybersource.com/content/dam/documents/campaign/fraud-report/global-fraud-report-2024.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **message/document/transaction/employee**. Decision: **review, coach, discipline, remediate**. Label/outcome: **breaches, audit findings, investigation yield**. Horizon: **daily**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from thousands to hundreds of thousands of daily or weekly decisions; value concentrates where senior decision latency or scarce expertise matters.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: payment gateway, order management, device/session telemetry, chargeback systems, case management, identity/KYC, graph store. In practice this data exists unevenly and usually needs standardization; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to review, coach, discipline, remediate; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are breaches, audit findings, investigation yield, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium: labels, causality, process standardization, or integration are often the bottleneck. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Payments emphasize millisecond decisions and false-positive cost; insurance and public-sector fraud emphasize investigation yield; internal fraud emphasizes audit evidence and controls.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to cybersecurity and compliance; fraud use cases decide monetary approval, investigation, or recovery, whereas cyber use cases decide technical containment.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in pilots but less consistently scaled: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: Mastercard reported average fraud-detection-rate improvement of 20%, up to 300% in some cases, and false-positive reduction of more than 85%; CyberSource reported ecommerce merchants use one to two AI/ML fraud tools on average. [Mastercard Decision Intelligence Pro 2024 announcement](https://www.mastercard.com/us/en/news-and-trends/press/2024/february/mastercard-supercharges-consumer-protection-with-gen-ai.html); [2024 Global eCommerce Payments & Fraud Report](https://www.cybersource.com/content/dam/documents/campaign/fraud-report/global-fraud-report-2024.pdf)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are adversarial adaptation, false positives that block good customers, delayed labels, investigation bottlenecks, and weak feedback from chargebacks or SAR outcomes. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — NLP: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [ACFE 2024 Report to the Nations](https://www.acfe.com/-/media/files/acfe/pdfs/rttn/2024/2024-report-to-the-nations.pdf); [2024 Global eCommerce Payments & Fraud Report](https://www.cybersource.com/content/dam/documents/campaign/fraud-report/global-fraud-report-2024.pdf); [Mastercard Decision Intelligence Pro 2024 announcement](https://www.mastercard.com/us/en/news-and-trends/press/2024/february/mastercard-supercharges-consumer-protection-with-gen-ai.html); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Contract-clause risk review

1. **Name + aliases** — Contract-clause risk review; aliases include clause deviation, redlining risk, contract compliance.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which clause, obligation, or negotiation point creates business or legal risk. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: legal. Outcome owner: general counsel, legal operations, contracting, compliance, records, discovery counsel.
4. **Economic pool** — Contract and document value is leakage and labor: KPMG reported more than 9% average contract-value leakage and 4.2% of contract value spent on avoidable post-award management. [KPMG contracting-process study](https://assets.kpmg.com/content/dam/kpmg/dk/pdf/Acor/2021/07/can-the-contracting-process-improve-without-an-owner.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **contract/clause/obligation**. Decision: **redline, escalate, accept, track**. Label/outcome: **cycle time, risk exceptions, leakage**. Horizon: **per contract**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from thousands to hundreds of thousands of daily or weekly decisions; value concentrates in large portfolios, shared services, or high-cost workflows.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: CLM, document management, e-discovery, email, DMS, policy systems, matter management, regulatory feeds. In practice this data often exists but needs cleaning; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to redline, escalate, accept, track; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are cycle time, risk exceptions, leakage, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium-high: data usually exists but requires integration, standard definitions, and human-in-the-loop workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — High-volume contracting emphasizes extraction and playbooks; litigation emphasizes recall and privilege; regulated operations emphasize evidence, audit trail, and defensible workflow.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to finance, procurement, and compliance; legal/document use cases own interpretation, obligation, privilege, and defensibility.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in scaled programs and advanced pilots: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: JPMorgan’s COiN was reported to save 360,000 hours of annual legal and loan-agreement review work; KPMG found contracting value leakage above 9%, creating a measurable target pool. [ABA Journal JPMorgan COiN report](https://www.abajournal.com/news/article/jpmorgan_chase_uses_tech_to_save_360000_hours_of_annual_work_by_lawyers_and); [KPMG contracting-process study](https://assets.kpmg.com/content/dam/kpmg/dk/pdf/Acor/2021/07/can-the-contracting-process-improve-without-an-owner.pdf)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are poor OCR, clause ambiguity, privilege risk, nonstandard templates, missing human review, and inability to prove defensible process. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Extraction: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [KPMG contracting-process study](https://assets.kpmg.com/content/dam/kpmg/dk/pdf/Acor/2021/07/can-the-contracting-process-improve-without-an-owner.pdf); [ABA Journal JPMorgan COiN report](https://www.abajournal.com/news/article/jpmorgan_chase_uses_tech_to_save_360000_hours_of_annual_work_by_lawyers_and); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

## F. Supply chain, operations & logistics

### Demand forecasting

1. **Name + aliases** — Demand forecasting; aliases include sales forecast, consumption forecast, demand sensing.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is how much demand will occur by item, location, segment, and time. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: operations. Outcome owner: COO, chief supply chain officer, demand planning, inventory, procurement, operations excellence.
4. **Economic pool** — Inventory and supply-chain decisions govern a large working-capital and service pool: IHL/Sensormatic estimated worldwide retail inventory distortion at $1.77 trillion in 2023, while McKinsey reports AI forecasting can reduce errors by 20% to 50% and lost sales/product unavailability by up to 65%. [IHL/Sensormatic 2023 inventory distortion study](https://www.sensormatic.com/resources/rp/2023/ihl-inventory-distortion-study); [McKinsey AI-driven operations forecasting](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/operations/our-insights/ai-driven-operations-forecasting-in-data-light-environments)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **SKU/location/customer/time**. Decision: **produce, buy, allocate, staff**. Label/outcome: **forecast error, service, waste**. Horizon: **daily/weekly**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: ERP, MRP, WMS, TMS, POS, order management, supplier portals, demand-planning, IoT/telemetry. In practice this data commonly exists in usable form; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to produce, buy, allocate, staff; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are forecast error, service, waste, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — High: data and labels are usually captured in operational systems, and the action can be wired into existing workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Retail/CPG emphasizes SKU-store demand and promotions; industrial firms emphasize spares and capacity; healthcare emphasizes service levels and expiration; e-commerce emphasizes promise speed and returns.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to finance working capital, sales forecasting, and manufacturing scheduling; supply-chain use cases turn forecasts into physical inventory and capacity actions.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is deployed at scale in many data-mature enterprises: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: DHL reported 23% storage-space reduction, +10% forecast-accuracy improvement, and more than €4 million working-capital savings; McKinsey reported AI forecasting can cut forecasting errors 20% to 50%. [DHL analytics case-study deck](https://www.dhl.com/content/dam/dhl/global/core/documents/pdf/glo-core-analytics-breakout.pdf); [McKinsey AI-driven operations forecasting](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/operations/our-insights/ai-driven-operations-forecasting-in-data-light-environments)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are promotion and stockout bias in history, sparse demand for long-tail SKUs, planners overriding black-box recommendations, and lack of execution integration into ERP/replenishment. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Forecasting: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [IHL/Sensormatic 2023 inventory distortion study](https://www.sensormatic.com/resources/rp/2023/ihl-inventory-distortion-study); [McKinsey AI-driven operations forecasting](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/operations/our-insights/ai-driven-operations-forecasting-in-data-light-environments); [DHL analytics case-study deck](https://www.dhl.com/content/dam/dhl/global/core/documents/pdf/glo-core-analytics-breakout.pdf); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).
