### Supply planning

1. **Name + aliases** — Supply planning; aliases include capacity planning, constraint planning, production planning.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is how supply, capacity, materials, and labor should be balanced against demand. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: operations. Outcome owner: COO, chief supply chain officer, demand planning, inventory, procurement, operations excellence.
4. **Economic pool** — Inventory and supply-chain decisions govern a large working-capital and service pool: IHL/Sensormatic estimated worldwide retail inventory distortion at $1.77 trillion in 2023, while McKinsey reports AI forecasting can reduce errors by 20% to 50% and lost sales/product unavailability by up to 65%. [IHL/Sensormatic 2023 inventory distortion study](https://www.sensormatic.com/resources/rp/2023/ihl-inventory-distortion-study); [McKinsey AI-driven operations forecasting](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/operations/our-insights/ai-driven-operations-forecasting-in-data-light-environments)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **plant/line/material/time**. Decision: **build plan, capacity plan, expedite**. Label/outcome: **OTIF, cost, utilization**. Horizon: **weekly/monthly**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from thousands to hundreds of thousands of daily or weekly decisions; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: ERP, MRP, WMS, TMS, POS, order management, supplier portals, demand-planning, IoT/telemetry. In practice this data often exists but needs cleaning; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to build plan, capacity plan, expedite; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are OTIF, cost, utilization, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium-high: data usually exists but requires integration, standard definitions, and human-in-the-loop workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Retail/CPG emphasizes SKU-store demand and promotions; industrial firms emphasize spares and capacity; healthcare emphasizes service levels and expiration; e-commerce emphasizes promise speed and returns.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to finance working capital, sales forecasting, and manufacturing scheduling; supply-chain use cases turn forecasts into physical inventory and capacity actions.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in scaled programs and advanced pilots: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: DHL reported 23% storage-space reduction, +10% forecast-accuracy improvement, and more than €4 million working-capital savings; McKinsey reported AI forecasting can cut forecasting errors 20% to 50%. [DHL analytics case-study deck](https://www.dhl.com/content/dam/dhl/global/core/documents/pdf/glo-core-analytics-breakout.pdf); [McKinsey AI-driven operations forecasting](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/operations/our-insights/ai-driven-operations-forecasting-in-data-light-environments)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are promotion and stockout bias in history, sparse demand for long-tail SKUs, planners overriding black-box recommendations, and lack of execution integration into ERP/replenishment. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Optimization: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [IHL/Sensormatic 2023 inventory distortion study](https://www.sensormatic.com/resources/rp/2023/ihl-inventory-distortion-study); [McKinsey AI-driven operations forecasting](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/operations/our-insights/ai-driven-operations-forecasting-in-data-light-environments); [DHL analytics case-study deck](https://www.dhl.com/content/dam/dhl/global/core/documents/pdf/glo-core-analytics-breakout.pdf); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Procurement spend analytics

1. **Name + aliases** — Procurement spend analytics; aliases include spend classification, category analytics, tail-spend analytics.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is where enterprise spend is going and which categories or suppliers present savings or compliance opportunities. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: procurement. Outcome owner: CFO, controller, treasurer, FP&A, credit, AP/AR, or shared-services leader.
4. **Economic pool** — Finance automation value comes from working capital, payment risk, and labor: PwC estimated €1.56 trillion of global excess working capital in 2024/25, and AFP found 79% of organizations experienced actual or attempted payments fraud in 2024. [PwC Working Capital Study 2024/25](https://www.pwc.co.uk/services/value-creation/insights/working-capital-study/2024.html); [2025 AFP Payments Fraud and Control Survey](https://www.truist.com/content/dam/truist-bank/us/en/documents/info/cci/2025-afp-payments-fraud-control-survey-report-key-highlights.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **supplier/category/invoice/PO**. Decision: **source, renegotiate, consolidate, enforce policy**. Label/outcome: **savings, compliance, leakage**. Horizon: **monthly/quarterly**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates in large portfolios, shared services, or high-cost workflows.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: ERP, AP/AR, treasury, TMS, bank statements, expense management, GL, close/reconciliation tools, procurement systems. In practice this data commonly exists in usable form; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to source, renegotiate, consolidate, enforce policy; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are savings, compliance, leakage, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — High: data and labels are usually captured in operational systems, and the action can be wired into existing workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Large enterprises have ERP/AP/AR history; banks and insurers add regulated model governance; smaller firms often lack clean labels and standardized workflows.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to procurement, risk, and operations; finance use cases own cash, controls, close, and financial planning outcomes.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is deployed at scale in many data-mature enterprises: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: Genpact reported an enterprise AP program reaching 80% touchless processing, while HighRadius reported Staples reduced bad debt by 20% using collections automation. [Genpact 80% touchless AP case study](https://www.genpact.com/case-studies/from-manual-to-80-percent-touchless-accounts-payable-at-enterprise-scale); [HighRadius Staples collections case study](https://www.highradius.com/resources/Radiance/20-reduction-in-bad-debt-staples-collections-automation-journey-with-ai/)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are inconsistent master data, unclear approval authority, exception-heavy processes, audit concerns, and automating broken controls; payment-fraud prevalence and working-capital scale make false positives and control design material. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Classification: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [PwC Working Capital Study 2024/25](https://www.pwc.co.uk/services/value-creation/insights/working-capital-study/2024.html); [2025 AFP Payments Fraud and Control Survey](https://www.truist.com/content/dam/truist-bank/us/en/documents/info/cci/2025-afp-payments-fraud-control-survey-report-key-highlights.pdf); [Genpact 80% touchless AP case study](https://www.genpact.com/case-studies/from-manual-to-80-percent-touchless-accounts-payable-at-enterprise-scale); [HighRadius Staples collections case study](https://www.highradius.com/resources/Radiance/20-reduction-in-bad-debt-staples-collections-automation-journey-with-ai/); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Supplier risk & selection

1. **Name + aliases** — Supplier risk & selection; aliases include supplier risk scoring, supplier performance prediction.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which suppliers are likely to fail, delay, violate policy, or create exposure. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: procurement. Outcome owner: COO, chief supply chain officer, demand planning, inventory, procurement, operations excellence.
4. **Economic pool** — Inventory and supply-chain decisions govern a large working-capital and service pool: IHL/Sensormatic estimated worldwide retail inventory distortion at $1.77 trillion in 2023, while McKinsey reports AI forecasting can reduce errors by 20% to 50% and lost sales/product unavailability by up to 65%. [IHL/Sensormatic 2023 inventory distortion study](https://www.sensormatic.com/resources/rp/2023/ihl-inventory-distortion-study); [McKinsey AI-driven operations forecasting](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/operations/our-insights/ai-driven-operations-forecasting-in-data-light-environments)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **supplier/part/category**. Decision: **dual-source, audit, qualify, avoid**. Label/outcome: **disruptions, OTIF, quality, ESG risk**. Horizon: **weekly/monthly**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from hundreds to thousands of recurring decisions; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: ERP, MRP, WMS, TMS, POS, order management, supplier portals, demand-planning, IoT/telemetry. In practice this data exists unevenly and usually needs standardization; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to dual-source, audit, qualify, avoid; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are disruptions, OTIF, quality, ESG risk, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium: labels, causality, process standardization, or integration are often the bottleneck. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Retail/CPG emphasizes SKU-store demand and promotions; industrial firms emphasize spares and capacity; healthcare emphasizes service levels and expiration; e-commerce emphasizes promise speed and returns.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to finance working capital, sales forecasting, and manufacturing scheduling; supply-chain use cases turn forecasts into physical inventory and capacity actions.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in pilots but less consistently scaled: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: DHL reported 23% storage-space reduction, +10% forecast-accuracy improvement, and more than €4 million working-capital savings; McKinsey reported AI forecasting can cut forecasting errors 20% to 50%. [DHL analytics case-study deck](https://www.dhl.com/content/dam/dhl/global/core/documents/pdf/glo-core-analytics-breakout.pdf); [McKinsey AI-driven operations forecasting](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/operations/our-insights/ai-driven-operations-forecasting-in-data-light-environments)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are promotion and stockout bias in history, sparse demand for long-tail SKUs, planners overriding black-box recommendations, and lack of execution integration into ERP/replenishment. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Scoring: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [IHL/Sensormatic 2023 inventory distortion study](https://www.sensormatic.com/resources/rp/2023/ihl-inventory-distortion-study); [McKinsey AI-driven operations forecasting](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/operations/our-insights/ai-driven-operations-forecasting-in-data-light-environments); [DHL analytics case-study deck](https://www.dhl.com/content/dam/dhl/global/core/documents/pdf/glo-core-analytics-breakout.pdf); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Price / contract leakage detection

1. **Name + aliases** — Price / contract leakage detection; aliases include procurement leakage, maverick spend, price variance.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is where buying behavior violates negotiated price, terms, or preferred supplier rules. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: procurement. Outcome owner: CFO, controller, treasurer, FP&A, credit, AP/AR, or shared-services leader.
4. **Economic pool** — Finance automation value comes from working capital, payment risk, and labor: PwC estimated €1.56 trillion of global excess working capital in 2024/25, and AFP found 79% of organizations experienced actual or attempted payments fraud in 2024. [PwC Working Capital Study 2024/25](https://www.pwc.co.uk/services/value-creation/insights/working-capital-study/2024.html); [2025 AFP Payments Fraud and Control Survey](https://www.truist.com/content/dam/truist-bank/us/en/documents/info/cci/2025-afp-payments-fraud-control-survey-report-key-highlights.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **invoice/PO/contract/supplier**. Decision: **recover, correct price, block order, renegotiate**. Label/outcome: **savings leakage, compliance**. Horizon: **daily/monthly**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates in large portfolios, shared services, or high-cost workflows.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: ERP, AP/AR, treasury, TMS, bank statements, expense management, GL, close/reconciliation tools, procurement systems. In practice this data commonly exists in usable form; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to recover, correct price, block order, renegotiate; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are savings leakage, compliance, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — High: data and labels are usually captured in operational systems, and the action can be wired into existing workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Large enterprises have ERP/AP/AR history; banks and insurers add regulated model governance; smaller firms often lack clean labels and standardized workflows.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to procurement, risk, and operations; finance use cases own cash, controls, close, and financial planning outcomes.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is deployed at scale in many data-mature enterprises: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: Genpact reported an enterprise AP program reaching 80% touchless processing, while HighRadius reported Staples reduced bad debt by 20% using collections automation. [Genpact 80% touchless AP case study](https://www.genpact.com/case-studies/from-manual-to-80-percent-touchless-accounts-payable-at-enterprise-scale); [HighRadius Staples collections case study](https://www.highradius.com/resources/Radiance/20-reduction-in-bad-debt-staples-collections-automation-journey-with-ai/)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are inconsistent master data, unclear approval authority, exception-heavy processes, audit concerns, and automating broken controls; payment-fraud prevalence and working-capital scale make false positives and control design material. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Anomaly: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [PwC Working Capital Study 2024/25](https://www.pwc.co.uk/services/value-creation/insights/working-capital-study/2024.html); [2025 AFP Payments Fraud and Control Survey](https://www.truist.com/content/dam/truist-bank/us/en/documents/info/cci/2025-afp-payments-fraud-control-survey-report-key-highlights.pdf); [Genpact 80% touchless AP case study](https://www.genpact.com/case-studies/from-manual-to-80-percent-touchless-accounts-payable-at-enterprise-scale); [HighRadius Staples collections case study](https://www.highradius.com/resources/Radiance/20-reduction-in-bad-debt-staples-collections-automation-journey-with-ai/); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Route optimization

1. **Name + aliases** — Route optimization; aliases include vehicle routing, dispatch optimization, last-mile routing.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which sequence of stops and vehicles minimizes cost while meeting service constraints. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: logistics. Outcome owner: VP logistics, transportation, field service, fleet operations, fulfillment leader.
4. **Economic pool** — Logistics value appears in route cost, capacity, service reliability, and delay avoidance; DHL reported a demand-and-inventory analytics case with 23% storage-space reduction, +10% forecast-accuracy improvement, and more than €4 million working-capital savings. [DHL analytics case-study deck](https://www.dhl.com/content/dam/dhl/global/core/documents/pdf/glo-core-analytics-breakout.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **order/vehicle/stop/route**. Decision: **assign vehicle, route driver, reschedule**. Label/outcome: **miles, fuel, on-time delivery**. Horizon: **real time/daily**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: TMS, WMS, route planning, telematics, GPS, order management, customer notification, driver apps. In practice this data commonly exists in usable form; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to assign vehicle, route driver, reschedule; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are miles, fuel, on-time delivery, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — High: data and labels are usually captured in operational systems, and the action can be wired into existing workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Parcel and food delivery decide in real time; freight and ocean logistics optimize multi-day networks; field service adds technician skill, parts, and customer-access constraints.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to order promising, customer notifications, and fleet maintenance; logistics owns movement, route, ETA, and utilization decisions.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is deployed at scale in many data-mature enterprises: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: BCG found only 26% of companies had moved beyond proofs of concept to tangible value in 2024, and future-built companies in 2025 achieved five times the revenue increases and three times the cost reductions of others. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [BCG 2025 AI value-gap study](https://www.bcg.com/publications/2025/are-you-generating-value-from-ai-the-widening-gap)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are stale traffic/constraint data, driver noncompliance, unrealistic promises, exception storms, and local optimization that hurts network cost. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Optimization: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [DHL analytics case-study deck](https://www.dhl.com/content/dam/dhl/global/core/documents/pdf/glo-core-analytics-breakout.pdf); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Warehouse slotting & labor planning

Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are pick rate, overtime, service, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — High: data and labels are usually captured in operational systems, and the action can be wired into existing workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Retail/CPG emphasizes SKU-store demand and promotions; industrial firms emphasize spares and capacity; healthcare emphasizes service levels and expiration; e-commerce emphasizes promise speed and returns.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to finance working capital, sales forecasting, and manufacturing scheduling; supply-chain use cases turn forecasts into physical inventory and capacity actions.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is deployed at scale in many data-mature enterprises: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: DHL reported 23% storage-space reduction, +10% forecast-accuracy improvement, and more than €4 million working-capital savings; McKinsey reported AI forecasting can cut forecasting errors 20% to 50%. [DHL analytics case-study deck](https://www.dhl.com/content/dam/dhl/global/core/documents/pdf/glo-core-analytics-breakout.pdf); [McKinsey AI-driven operations forecasting](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/operations/our-insights/ai-driven-operations-forecasting-in-data-light-environments)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are promotion and stockout bias in history, sparse demand for long-tail SKUs, planners overriding black-box recommendations, and lack of execution integration into ERP/replenishment. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Optimization: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [IHL/Sensormatic 2023 inventory distortion study](https://www.sensormatic.com/resources/rp/2023/ihl-inventory-distortion-study); [McKinsey AI-driven operations forecasting](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/operations/our-insights/ai-driven-operations-forecasting-in-data-light-environments); [DHL analytics case-study deck](https://www.dhl.com/content/dam/dhl/global/core/documents/pdf/glo-core-analytics-breakout.pdf); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Order-promise / fulfillment optimization

1. **Name + aliases** — Order-promise / fulfillment optimization; aliases include available-to-promise, delivery promise, order orchestration.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which fulfillment node and promise date should be offered or selected. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: operations. Outcome owner: COO, chief supply chain officer, demand planning, inventory, procurement, operations excellence.
4. **Economic pool** — Inventory and supply-chain decisions govern a large working-capital and service pool: IHL/Sensormatic estimated worldwide retail inventory distortion at $1.77 trillion in 2023, while McKinsey reports AI forecasting can reduce errors by 20% to 50% and lost sales/product unavailability by up to 65%. [IHL/Sensormatic 2023 inventory distortion study](https://www.sensormatic.com/resources/rp/2023/ihl-inventory-distortion-study); [McKinsey AI-driven operations forecasting](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/operations/our-insights/ai-driven-operations-forecasting-in-data-light-environments)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **order/customer/node/SKU**. Decision: **promise, allocate, split, expedite**. Label/outcome: **OTIF, cost/order, conversion**. Horizon: **real time**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from thousands to hundreds of thousands of daily or weekly decisions; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: ERP, MRP, WMS, TMS, POS, order management, supplier portals, demand-planning, IoT/telemetry. In practice this data often exists but needs cleaning; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to promise, allocate, split, expedite; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are OTIF, cost/order, conversion, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium-high: data usually exists but requires integration, standard definitions, and human-in-the-loop workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Retail/CPG emphasizes SKU-store demand and promotions; industrial firms emphasize spares and capacity; healthcare emphasizes service levels and expiration; e-commerce emphasizes promise speed and returns.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to finance working capital, sales forecasting, and manufacturing scheduling; supply-chain use cases turn forecasts into physical inventory and capacity actions.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in scaled programs and advanced pilots: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: DHL reported 23% storage-space reduction, +10% forecast-accuracy improvement, and more than €4 million working-capital savings; McKinsey reported AI forecasting can cut forecasting errors 20% to 50%. [DHL analytics case-study deck](https://www.dhl.com/content/dam/dhl/global/core/documents/pdf/glo-core-analytics-breakout.pdf); [McKinsey AI-driven operations forecasting](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/operations/our-insights/ai-driven-operations-forecasting-in-data-light-environments)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are promotion and stockout bias in history, sparse demand for long-tail SKUs, planners overriding black-box recommendations, and lack of execution integration into ERP/replenishment. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Optimization: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [IHL/Sensormatic 2023 inventory distortion study](https://www.sensormatic.com/resources/rp/2023/ihl-inventory-distortion-study); [McKinsey AI-driven operations forecasting](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/operations/our-insights/ai-driven-operations-forecasting-in-data-light-environments); [DHL analytics case-study deck](https://www.dhl.com/content/dam/dhl/global/core/documents/pdf/glo-core-analytics-breakout.pdf); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### S&OP support

1. **Name + aliases** — S&OP support; aliases include IBP analytics, consensus planning, plan exception analytics.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which demand, supply, and financial planning assumptions need executive resolution. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: operations. Outcome owner: COO, chief supply chain officer, demand planning, inventory, procurement, operations excellence.
4. **Economic pool** — Inventory and supply-chain decisions govern a large working-capital and service pool: IHL/Sensormatic estimated worldwide retail inventory distortion at $1.77 trillion in 2023, while McKinsey reports AI forecasting can reduce errors by 20% to 50% and lost sales/product unavailability by up to 65%. [IHL/Sensormatic 2023 inventory distortion study](https://www.sensormatic.com/resources/rp/2023/ihl-inventory-distortion-study); [McKinsey AI-driven operations forecasting](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/operations/our-insights/ai-driven-operations-forecasting-in-data-light-environments)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **plan/driver/exception**. Decision: **approve scenario, rebalance plan, escalate**. Label/outcome: **plan adherence, bias, service, margin**. Horizon: **monthly**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from thousands to hundreds of thousands of daily or weekly decisions; value concentrates in large portfolios, shared services, or high-cost workflows.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: ERP, MRP, WMS, TMS, POS, order management, supplier portals, demand-planning, IoT/telemetry. In practice this data exists unevenly and usually needs standardization; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to approve scenario, rebalance plan, escalate; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are plan adherence, bias, service, margin, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium: labels, causality, process standardization, or integration are often the bottleneck. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Retail/CPG emphasizes SKU-store demand and promotions; industrial firms emphasize spares and capacity; healthcare emphasizes service levels and expiration; e-commerce emphasizes promise speed and returns.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to finance working capital, sales forecasting, and manufacturing scheduling; supply-chain use cases turn forecasts into physical inventory and capacity actions.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in pilots but less consistently scaled: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)

## G. Manufacturing & industrial

### Predictive maintenance

1. **Name + aliases** — Predictive maintenance; aliases include condition-based maintenance, maintenance prognostics.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which asset is likely to fail or degrade and when maintenance should occur. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: operations. Outcome owner: plant manager, reliability leader, maintenance, operations, manufacturing excellence.
4. **Economic pool** — Industrial reliability has a large loss-avoidance pool: Siemens estimated unplanned downtime costs the world’s 500 biggest companies $1.4 trillion annually and 11% of revenue. [Siemens True Cost of Downtime 2024](https://assets.new.siemens.com/siemens/assets/api/uuid:1b43afb5-2d07-47f7-9eb7-893fe7d0bc59/TCOD-2024_original.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **machine/component/asset**. Decision: **inspect, repair, schedule downtime, order parts**. Label/outcome: **downtime, MTBF, maintenance cost**. Horizon: **real time/daily**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: MES, SCADA, historian, CMMS/EAM, PLC/sensor data, quality systems, work orders, ERP. In practice this data commonly exists in usable form; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to inspect, repair, schedule downtime, order parts; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are downtime, MTBF, maintenance cost, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — High: data and labels are usually captured in operational systems, and the action can be wired into existing workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Discrete manufacturing emphasizes parts and visual defects; process manufacturing emphasizes yield, recipe, and sensor control; asset-heavy industries emphasize uptime and safety.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to supply planning and quality; manufacturing use cases optimize physical assets, lines, recipes, and maintenance actions.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is deployed at scale in many data-mature enterprises: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: BlueScope avoided about 2,000 hours of unplanned downtime and prevented 53 complete process interruptions with predictive maintenance; Google DeepMind cut data-center cooling energy by up to 40%. [Siemens BlueScope predictive-maintenance case study](https://news.siemens.com/en-us/bluescope-predictive-maintenance/); [Google DeepMind data-center cooling case study](https://deepmind.google/blog/deepmind-ai-reduces-google-data-centre-cooling-bill-by-40/)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are insufficient failure examples, sensor drift, missing maintenance history, unplanned process changes, and no authority to take equipment down for preventive work. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Prediction: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [Siemens True Cost of Downtime 2024](https://assets.new.siemens.com/siemens/assets/api/uuid:1b43afb5-2d07-47f7-9eb7-893fe7d0bc59/TCOD-2024_original.pdf); [Siemens BlueScope predictive-maintenance case study](https://news.siemens.com/en-us/bluescope-predictive-maintenance/); [Google DeepMind data-center cooling case study](https://deepmind.google/blog/deepmind-ai-reduces-google-data-centre-cooling-bill-by-40/); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Asset-failure prediction

1. **Name + aliases** — Asset-failure prediction; aliases include failure risk, remaining useful life, reliability prediction.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which asset, fleet unit, or component has elevated failure probability over a future horizon. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: operations. Outcome owner: plant manager, reliability leader, maintenance, operations, manufacturing excellence.
4. **Economic pool** — Industrial reliability has a large loss-avoidance pool: Siemens estimated unplanned downtime costs the world’s 500 biggest companies $1.4 trillion annually and 11% of revenue. [Siemens True Cost of Downtime 2024](https://assets.new.siemens.com/siemens/assets/api/uuid:1b43afb5-2d07-47f7-9eb7-893fe7d0bc59/TCOD-2024_original.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **asset/component/sensor stream**. Decision: **replace, derate, monitor, dispatch**. Label/outcome: **failures, safety, downtime**. Horizon: **real time/daily**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from thousands to hundreds of thousands of daily or weekly decisions; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: MES, SCADA, historian, CMMS/EAM, PLC/sensor data, quality systems, work orders, ERP. In practice this data often exists but needs cleaning; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to replace, derate, monitor, dispatch; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are failures, safety, downtime, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium-high: data usually exists but requires integration, standard definitions, and human-in-the-loop workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Discrete manufacturing emphasizes parts and visual defects; process manufacturing emphasizes yield, recipe, and sensor control; asset-heavy industries emphasize uptime and safety.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to supply planning and quality; manufacturing use cases optimize physical assets, lines, recipes, and maintenance actions.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in scaled programs and advanced pilots: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: BlueScope avoided about 2,000 hours of unplanned downtime and prevented 53 complete process interruptions with predictive maintenance; Google DeepMind cut data-center cooling energy by up to 40%. [Siemens BlueScope predictive-maintenance case study](https://news.siemens.com/en-us/bluescope-predictive-maintenance/); [Google DeepMind data-center cooling case study](https://deepmind.google/blog/deepmind-ai-reduces-google-data-centre-cooling-bill-by-40/)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are insufficient failure examples, sensor drift, missing maintenance history, unplanned process changes, and no authority to take equipment down for preventive work. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Survival: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [Siemens True Cost of Downtime 2024](https://assets.new.siemens.com/siemens/assets/api/uuid:1b43afb5-2d07-47f7-9eb7-893fe7d0bc59/TCOD-2024_original.pdf); [Siemens BlueScope predictive-maintenance case study](https://news.siemens.com/en-us/bluescope-predictive-maintenance/); [Google DeepMind data-center cooling case study](https://deepmind.google/blog/deepmind-ai-reduces-google-data-centre-cooling-bill-by-40/); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Quality inspection

1. **Name + aliases** — Quality inspection; aliases include visual inspection, defect detection, automated QA.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which product, part, image, or batch is defective or out of specification. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: quality. Outcome owner: quality leader, plant quality, manufacturing engineering, regulatory quality.
4. **Economic pool** — Quality and process decisions protect yield, warranty, scrap, and customer acceptance; downtime and process losses are large enough that Siemens placed global top-500 downtime losses at $1.4 trillion annually. [Siemens True Cost of Downtime 2024](https://assets.new.siemens.com/siemens/assets/api/uuid:1b43afb5-2d07-47f7-9eb7-893fe7d0bc59/TCOD-2024_original.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **part/image/batch**. Decision: **reject, rework, adjust process, release**. Label/outcome: **defect escape, yield, scrap**. Horizon: **real time**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: QMS, MES, vision systems, lab systems, sensors, nonconformance records, warranty/returns, ERP. In practice this data commonly exists in usable form; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to reject, rework, adjust process, release; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are defect escape, yield, scrap, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — High: data and labels are usually captured in operational systems, and the action can be wired into existing workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Regulated sectors such as pharma, aerospace, and medical devices require validation, traceability, and explainability; high-volume consumer manufacturing emphasizes speed and defect escape.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to predictive maintenance and process optimization; quality owns defect, yield, release, and conformance decisions.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is deployed at scale in many data-mature enterprises: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: BCG found only 26% of companies had moved beyond proofs of concept to tangible value in 2024, and future-built companies in 2025 achieved five times the revenue increases and three times the cost reductions of others. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [BCG 2025 AI value-gap study](https://www.bcg.com/publications/2025/are-you-generating-value-from-ai-the-widening-gap)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are changing lighting/camera setups, defect rarity, weak golden labels, unmanaged false rejects, and lack of closed-loop process control. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Vision: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [Siemens True Cost of Downtime 2024](https://assets.new.siemens.com/siemens/assets/api/uuid:1b43afb5-2d07-47f7-9eb7-893fe7d0bc59/TCOD-2024_original.pdf); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Yield / process optimization

1. **Name + aliases** — Yield / process optimization; aliases include process control, recipe optimization, throughput optimization.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which controllable process settings improve yield, throughput, or consistency. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: operations. Outcome owner: quality leader, plant quality, manufacturing engineering, regulatory quality.
4. **Economic pool** — Quality and process decisions protect yield, warranty, scrap, and customer acceptance; downtime and process losses are large enough that Siemens placed global top-500 downtime losses at $1.4 trillion annually. [Siemens True Cost of Downtime 2024](https://assets.new.siemens.com/siemens/assets/api/uuid:1b43afb5-2d07-47f7-9eb7-893fe7d0bc59/TCOD-2024_original.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **batch/line/process parameter**. Decision: **adjust setpoint, recipe, speed, sequence**. Label/outcome: **yield, throughput, variance**. Horizon: **real time/shift**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from thousands to hundreds of thousands of daily or weekly decisions; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: QMS, MES, vision systems, lab systems, sensors, nonconformance records, warranty/returns, ERP. In practice this data often exists but needs cleaning; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to adjust setpoint, recipe, speed, sequence; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are yield, throughput, variance, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium-high: data usually exists but requires integration, standard definitions, and human-in-the-loop workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Regulated sectors such as pharma, aerospace, and medical devices require validation, traceability, and explainability; high-volume consumer manufacturing emphasizes speed and defect escape.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to predictive maintenance and process optimization; quality owns defect, yield, release, and conformance decisions.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in scaled programs and advanced pilots: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: BCG found only 26% of companies had moved beyond proofs of concept to tangible value in 2024, and future-built companies in 2025 achieved five times the revenue increases and three times the cost reductions of others. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [BCG 2025 AI value-gap study](https://www.bcg.com/publications/2025/are-you-generating-value-from-ai-the-widening-gap)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are changing lighting/camera setups, defect rarity, weak golden labels, unmanaged false rejects, and lack of closed-loop process control. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Optimization: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.

### Scrap & rework reduction

6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates in large portfolios, shared services, or high-cost workflows.
