### Energy optimization

1. **Name + aliases** — Energy optimization; aliases include industrial energy management, utility optimization, PUE optimization.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which operating settings minimize energy use without violating service, safety, or quality constraints. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: operations. Outcome owner: plant manager, reliability leader, maintenance, operations, manufacturing excellence.
4. **Economic pool** — Industrial reliability has a large loss-avoidance pool: Siemens estimated unplanned downtime costs the world’s 500 biggest companies $1.4 trillion annually and 11% of revenue. [Siemens True Cost of Downtime 2024](https://assets.new.siemens.com/siemens/assets/api/uuid:1b43afb5-2d07-47f7-9eb7-893fe7d0bc59/TCOD-2024_original.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **asset/site/hour/setpoint**. Decision: **adjust setpoint, load shift, maintain**. Label/outcome: **energy cost, emissions, uptime**. Horizon: **real time/hourly**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from thousands to hundreds of thousands of daily or weekly decisions; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: MES, SCADA, historian, CMMS/EAM, PLC/sensor data, quality systems, work orders, ERP. In practice this data often exists but needs cleaning; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to adjust setpoint, load shift, maintain; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are energy cost, emissions, uptime, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium-high: data usually exists but requires integration, standard definitions, and human-in-the-loop workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Discrete manufacturing emphasizes parts and visual defects; process manufacturing emphasizes yield, recipe, and sensor control; asset-heavy industries emphasize uptime and safety.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to supply planning and quality; manufacturing use cases optimize physical assets, lines, recipes, and maintenance actions.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in scaled programs and advanced pilots: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: BlueScope avoided about 2,000 hours of unplanned downtime and prevented 53 complete process interruptions with predictive maintenance; Google DeepMind cut data-center cooling energy by up to 40%. [Siemens BlueScope predictive-maintenance case study](https://news.siemens.com/en-us/bluescope-predictive-maintenance/); [Google DeepMind data-center cooling case study](https://deepmind.google/blog/deepmind-ai-reduces-google-data-centre-cooling-bill-by-40/)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are insufficient failure examples, sensor drift, missing maintenance history, unplanned process changes, and no authority to take equipment down for preventive work. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Optimization: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [Siemens True Cost of Downtime 2024](https://assets.new.siemens.com/siemens/assets/api/uuid:1b43afb5-2d07-47f7-9eb7-893fe7d0bc59/TCOD-2024_original.pdf); [Siemens BlueScope predictive-maintenance case study](https://news.siemens.com/en-us/bluescope-predictive-maintenance/); [Google DeepMind data-center cooling case study](https://deepmind.google/blog/deepmind-ai-reduces-google-data-centre-cooling-bill-by-40/); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Production scheduling

1. **Name + aliases** — Production scheduling; aliases include line scheduling, finite-capacity scheduling, sequencing.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which jobs should run on which line and in what order given constraints. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: operations. Outcome owner: COO, chief supply chain officer, demand planning, inventory, procurement, operations excellence.
4. **Economic pool** — Inventory and supply-chain decisions govern a large working-capital and service pool: IHL/Sensormatic estimated worldwide retail inventory distortion at $1.77 trillion in 2023, while McKinsey reports AI forecasting can reduce errors by 20% to 50% and lost sales/product unavailability by up to 65%. [IHL/Sensormatic 2023 inventory distortion study](https://www.sensormatic.com/resources/rp/2023/ihl-inventory-distortion-study); [McKinsey AI-driven operations forecasting](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/operations/our-insights/ai-driven-operations-forecasting-in-data-light-environments)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **job/line/material/shift**. Decision: **sequence, staff, expedite, changeover**. Label/outcome: **throughput, lateness, changeover**. Horizon: **daily/shift**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from thousands to hundreds of thousands of daily or weekly decisions; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: ERP, MRP, WMS, TMS, POS, order management, supplier portals, demand-planning, IoT/telemetry. In practice this data often exists but needs cleaning; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to sequence, staff, expedite, changeover; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are throughput, lateness, changeover, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium-high: data usually exists but requires integration, standard definitions, and human-in-the-loop workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Retail/CPG emphasizes SKU-store demand and promotions; industrial firms emphasize spares and capacity; healthcare emphasizes service levels and expiration; e-commerce emphasizes promise speed and returns.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to finance working capital, sales forecasting, and manufacturing scheduling; supply-chain use cases turn forecasts into physical inventory and capacity actions.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in scaled programs and advanced pilots: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: DHL reported 23% storage-space reduction, +10% forecast-accuracy improvement, and more than €4 million working-capital savings; McKinsey reported AI forecasting can cut forecasting errors 20% to 50%. [DHL analytics case-study deck](https://www.dhl.com/content/dam/dhl/global/core/documents/pdf/glo-core-analytics-breakout.pdf); [McKinsey AI-driven operations forecasting](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/operations/our-insights/ai-driven-operations-forecasting-in-data-light-environments)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are promotion and stockout bias in history, sparse demand for long-tail SKUs, planners overriding black-box recommendations, and lack of execution integration into ERP/replenishment. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Optimization: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [IHL/Sensormatic 2023 inventory distortion study](https://www.sensormatic.com/resources/rp/2023/ihl-inventory-distortion-study); [McKinsey AI-driven operations forecasting](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/operations/our-insights/ai-driven-operations-forecasting-in-data-light-environments); [DHL analytics case-study deck](https://www.dhl.com/content/dam/dhl/global/core/documents/pdf/glo-core-analytics-breakout.pdf); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Digital-twin simulation

1. **Name + aliases** — Digital-twin simulation; aliases include process twin, plant twin, scenario simulation.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is how a physical asset or process will behave under alternative operating scenarios. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: operations. Outcome owner: plant manager, reliability leader, maintenance, operations, manufacturing excellence.
4. **Economic pool** — Industrial reliability has a large loss-avoidance pool: Siemens estimated unplanned downtime costs the world’s 500 biggest companies $1.4 trillion annually and 11% of revenue. [Siemens True Cost of Downtime 2024](https://assets.new.siemens.com/siemens/assets/api/uuid:1b43afb5-2d07-47f7-9eb7-893fe7d0bc59/TCOD-2024_original.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **asset/process/scenario**. Decision: **simulate, derisk, optimize, train**. Label/outcome: **downtime, capital efficiency, safety**. Horizon: **project/continuous**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from hundreds to thousands of recurring decisions; value concentrates in large portfolios, shared services, or high-cost workflows.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: MES, SCADA, historian, CMMS/EAM, PLC/sensor data, quality systems, work orders, ERP. In practice this data exists unevenly and usually needs standardization; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to simulate, derisk, optimize, train; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are downtime, capital efficiency, safety, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium: labels, causality, process standardization, or integration are often the bottleneck. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Discrete manufacturing emphasizes parts and visual defects; process manufacturing emphasizes yield, recipe, and sensor control; asset-heavy industries emphasize uptime and safety.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to supply planning and quality; manufacturing use cases optimize physical assets, lines, recipes, and maintenance actions.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in pilots but less consistently scaled: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: BlueScope avoided about 2,000 hours of unplanned downtime and prevented 53 complete process interruptions with predictive maintenance; Google DeepMind cut data-center cooling energy by up to 40%. [Siemens BlueScope predictive-maintenance case study](https://news.siemens.com/en-us/bluescope-predictive-maintenance/); [Google DeepMind data-center cooling case study](https://deepmind.google/blog/deepmind-ai-reduces-google-data-centre-cooling-bill-by-40/)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are insufficient failure examples, sensor drift, missing maintenance history, unplanned process changes, and no authority to take equipment down for preventive work. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Simulation: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [Siemens True Cost of Downtime 2024](https://assets.new.siemens.com/siemens/assets/api/uuid:1b43afb5-2d07-47f7-9eb7-893fe7d0bc59/TCOD-2024_original.pdf); [Siemens BlueScope predictive-maintenance case study](https://news.siemens.com/en-us/bluescope-predictive-maintenance/); [Google DeepMind data-center cooling case study](https://deepmind.google/blog/deepmind-ai-reduces-google-data-centre-cooling-bill-by-40/); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

## H. IT, engineering & security

### Incident prediction & triage

1. **Name + aliases** — Incident prediction & triage; aliases include AIOps incident triage, outage prediction.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which IT event will become a material incident and who should respond. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: IT. Outcome owner: CIO, SRE/IT operations, platform engineering, service owners, cloud operations.
4. **Economic pool** — IT reliability has measurable outage economics: New Relic reported 62% of high-business-impact outages cost at least $1 million per hour, with median annual downtime of 77 hours. [New Relic 2024 observability forecast](https://newrelic.com/resources/report/observability-forecast/2024/state-of-observability/outages-downtime-cost)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **alert/service/event**. Decision: **prioritize, page, suppress, open incident**. Label/outcome: **MTTD, MTTR, outage cost**. Horizon: **real time**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: ITSM, observability/APM, logs, metrics, traces, CMDB, CI/CD, cloud billing, change management. In practice this data often exists but needs cleaning; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to prioritize, page, suppress, open incident; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are MTTD, MTTR, outage cost, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium-high: data usually exists but requires integration, standard definitions, and human-in-the-loop workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Cloud-native firms have dense telemetry and automation hooks; legacy enterprises have fragmented logs and change records; regulated firms require strict change control.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to cybersecurity and software engineering; IT owns service reliability, capacity, incident, and observability outcomes.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in scaled programs and advanced pilots: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: GitHub’s experiment found developers with Copilot completed a coding task 55.8% faster; McKinsey found generative AI can complete code documentation in half the time in its developer study. [Microsoft Research GitHub Copilot field experiment](https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/research/publication/the-impact-of-ai-on-developer-productivity-evidence-from-github-copilot/); [McKinsey developer-productivity study](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/tech-and-ai/our-insights/unleashing-developer-productivity-with-generative-ai)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are alert fatigue, topology gaps, untrusted automation, noisy labels, and teams measuring alert volume instead of downtime, MTTR, or customer impact. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Classification: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [New Relic 2024 observability forecast](https://newrelic.com/resources/report/observability-forecast/2024/state-of-observability/outages-downtime-cost); [Microsoft Research GitHub Copilot field experiment](https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/research/publication/the-impact-of-ai-on-developer-productivity-evidence-from-github-copilot/); [McKinsey developer-productivity study](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/tech-and-ai/our-insights/unleashing-developer-productivity-with-generative-ai); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Alert correlation & noise reduction

1. **Name + aliases** — Alert correlation & noise reduction; aliases include event correlation, deduplication, alert suppression.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which alerts represent the same incident or can be safely suppressed. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: IT. Outcome owner: CIO, SRE/IT operations, platform engineering, service owners, cloud operations.
4. **Economic pool** — IT reliability has measurable outage economics: New Relic reported 62% of high-business-impact outages cost at least $1 million per hour, with median annual downtime of 77 hours. [New Relic 2024 observability forecast](https://newrelic.com/resources/report/observability-forecast/2024/state-of-observability/outages-downtime-cost)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **alert/event/service**. Decision: **dedupe, correlate, suppress, group**. Label/outcome: **noise, pages, MTTR**. Horizon: **real time**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates in large portfolios, shared services, or high-cost workflows.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: ITSM, observability/APM, logs, metrics, traces, CMDB, CI/CD, cloud billing, change management. In practice this data commonly exists in usable form; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to dedupe, correlate, suppress, group; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Primary KPIs are noise, pages, MTTR, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.

### Code generation & review assistance

1. **Name + aliases** — Code generation & review assistance; aliases include developer copilot, code completion, PR review.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is how to help developers write, review, explain, and refactor code faster and safely. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: engineering. Outcome owner: CTO, VP engineering, platform engineering, developer productivity, QA.
4. **Economic pool** — Software-engineering value is labor productivity and time-to-market; McKinsey found genAI could complete documentation in half the time and code generation in nearly half the time in its developer study. [McKinsey developer-productivity study](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/tech-and-ai/our-insights/unleashing-developer-productivity-with-generative-ai)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **developer/task/repository/PR**. Decision: **suggest code, review, refactor, document**. Label/outcome: **cycle time, defects, productivity**. Horizon: **continuous**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: source control, CI/CD, issue tracker, code review, test management, documentation, artifact registry. In practice this data often exists but needs cleaning; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to suggest code, review, refactor, document; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are cycle time, defects, productivity, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium-high: data usually exists but requires integration, standard definitions, and human-in-the-loop workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Digital-native companies have rich repositories and CI/CD telemetry; regulated engineering organizations require secure code, IP controls, and human review.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to IT release risk and product management; software engineering owns code, tests, reviews, and developer throughput.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in scaled programs and advanced pilots: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: BCG found only 26% of companies had moved beyond proofs of concept to tangible value in 2024, and future-built companies in 2025 achieved five times the revenue increases and three times the cost reductions of others. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [BCG 2025 AI value-gap study](https://www.bcg.com/publications/2025/are-you-generating-value-from-ai-the-widening-gap)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are insecure or unreviewed generated code, IP/data leakage, low-context suggestions, productivity metrics that ignore rework, and uneven benefit by task complexity. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Generative: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [McKinsey developer-productivity study](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/tech-and-ai/our-insights/unleashing-developer-productivity-with-generative-ai); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Release-risk prediction

1. **Name + aliases** — Release-risk prediction; aliases include change failure prediction, deployment risk scoring.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which code changes or releases are likely to fail or create incidents. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: engineering. Outcome owner: CIO, SRE/IT operations, platform engineering, service owners, cloud operations.
4. **Economic pool** — IT reliability has measurable outage economics: New Relic reported 62% of high-business-impact outages cost at least $1 million per hour, with median annual downtime of 77 hours. [New Relic 2024 observability forecast](https://newrelic.com/resources/report/observability-forecast/2024/state-of-observability/outages-downtime-cost)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **commit/PR/build/release**. Decision: **hold, canary, rollback, add tests**. Label/outcome: **change failure rate, MTTR**. Horizon: **per commit/release**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from thousands to hundreds of thousands of daily or weekly decisions; value concentrates in large portfolios, shared services, or high-cost workflows.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: ITSM, observability/APM, logs, metrics, traces, CMDB, CI/CD, cloud billing, change management. In practice this data often exists but needs cleaning; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to hold, canary, rollback, add tests; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are change failure rate, MTTR, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium-high: data usually exists but requires integration, standard definitions, and human-in-the-loop workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Cloud-native firms have dense telemetry and automation hooks; legacy enterprises have fragmented logs and change records; regulated firms require strict change control.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to cybersecurity and software engineering; IT owns service reliability, capacity, incident, and observability outcomes.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in scaled programs and advanced pilots: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: GitHub’s experiment found developers with Copilot completed a coding task 55.8% faster; McKinsey found generative AI can complete code documentation in half the time in its developer study. [Microsoft Research GitHub Copilot field experiment](https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/research/publication/the-impact-of-ai-on-developer-productivity-evidence-from-github-copilot/); [McKinsey developer-productivity study](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/tech-and-ai/our-insights/unleashing-developer-productivity-with-generative-ai)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are alert fatigue, topology gaps, untrusted automation, noisy labels, and teams measuring alert volume instead of downtime, MTTR, or customer impact. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Scoring: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [New Relic 2024 observability forecast](https://newrelic.com/resources/report/observability-forecast/2024/state-of-observability/outages-downtime-cost); [Microsoft Research GitHub Copilot field experiment](https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/research/publication/the-impact-of-ai-on-developer-productivity-evidence-from-github-copilot/); [McKinsey developer-productivity study](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/tech-and-ai/our-insights/unleashing-developer-productivity-with-generative-ai); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Cybersecurity threat detection & response

1. **Name + aliases** — Cybersecurity threat detection & response; aliases include SIEM analytics, XDR detection, SOC automation.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which events indicate attack activity and what response should contain it. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: security. Outcome owner: CISO, SOC, vulnerability management, IAM, threat detection and response.
4. **Economic pool** — Cybersecurity value is loss avoidance and resilience: FBI IC3 recorded $16.6 billion in reported 2024 losses, and IBM placed the 2024 global average data-breach cost at $4.88 million. [FBI IC3 2024 Internet Crime Report](https://www.ic3.gov/AnnualReport/Reports/2024_ic3report.pdf); [IBM 2024 Cost of a Data Breach press release](https://newsroom.ibm.com/2024-07-30-ibm-report-escalating-data-breach-disruption-pushes-costs-to-new-highs)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **event/endpoint/user/network**. Decision: **alert, isolate, block, investigate**. Label/outcome: **dwell time, breach cost, false positives**. Horizon: **real time**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: SIEM, XDR/EDR, IAM, email security, vulnerability scanners, asset inventory, network telemetry, case management. In practice this data often exists but needs cleaning; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to alert, isolate, block, investigate; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are dwell time, breach cost, false positives, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium-high: data usually exists but requires integration, standard definitions, and human-in-the-loop workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Financial services and critical infrastructure face high regulatory exposure; ecommerce and SaaS emphasize account/session protection; healthcare emphasizes protected data and patient safety.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to fraud and IT operations; security owns threat, vulnerability, identity, and containment outcomes.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in scaled programs and advanced pilots: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: IBM reported organizations using extensive security AI and automation in prevention workflows had $2.2 million lower breach costs than those with no use; Mastercard reported fraud false positives could be reduced by more than 85%. [IBM 2024 Cost of a Data Breach press release](https://newsroom.ibm.com/2024-07-30-ibm-report-escalating-data-breach-disruption-pushes-costs-to-new-highs); [Mastercard Decision Intelligence Pro 2024 announcement](https://www.mastercard.com/us/en/news-and-trends/press/2024/february/mastercard-supercharges-consumer-protection-with-gen-ai.html)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are adversarial drift, encrypted or missing telemetry, false-positive overload, analyst workflow gaps, and unclear containment authority. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Detection: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [FBI IC3 2024 Internet Crime Report](https://www.ic3.gov/AnnualReport/Reports/2024_ic3report.pdf); [IBM 2024 Cost of a Data Breach press release](https://newsroom.ibm.com/2024-07-30-ibm-report-escalating-data-breach-disruption-pushes-costs-to-new-highs); [Mastercard Decision Intelligence Pro 2024 announcement](https://www.mastercard.com/us/en/news-and-trends/press/2024/february/mastercard-supercharges-consumer-protection-with-gen-ai.html); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Phishing detection

1. **Name + aliases** — Phishing detection; aliases include email threat detection, BEC detection.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which messages or domains are malicious, fraudulent, or impersonating trusted parties. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: security. Outcome owner: CISO, SOC, vulnerability management, IAM, threat detection and response.
4. **Economic pool** — Cybersecurity value is loss avoidance and resilience: FBI IC3 recorded $16.6 billion in reported 2024 losses, and IBM placed the 2024 global average data-breach cost at $4.88 million. [FBI IC3 2024 Internet Crime Report](https://www.ic3.gov/AnnualReport/Reports/2024_ic3report.pdf); [IBM 2024 Cost of a Data Breach press release](https://newsroom.ibm.com/2024-07-30-ibm-report-escalating-data-breach-disruption-pushes-costs-to-new-highs)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **email/domain/user click**. Decision: **quarantine, warn, block, train**. Label/outcome: **click rate, compromise, false positives**. Horizon: **real time**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates in large portfolios, shared services, or high-cost workflows.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: SIEM, XDR/EDR, IAM, email security, vulnerability scanners, asset inventory, network telemetry, case management. In practice this data commonly exists in usable form; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to quarantine, warn, block, train; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are click rate, compromise, false positives, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — High: data and labels are usually captured in operational systems, and the action can be wired into existing workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Financial services and critical infrastructure face high regulatory exposure; ecommerce and SaaS emphasize account/session protection; healthcare emphasizes protected data and patient safety.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to fraud and IT operations; security owns threat, vulnerability, identity, and containment outcomes.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is deployed at scale in many data-mature enterprises: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: IBM reported organizations using extensive security AI and automation in prevention workflows had $2.2 million lower breach costs than those with no use; Mastercard reported fraud false positives could be reduced by more than 85%. [IBM 2024 Cost of a Data Breach press release](https://newsroom.ibm.com/2024-07-30-ibm-report-escalating-data-breach-disruption-pushes-costs-to-new-highs); [Mastercard Decision Intelligence Pro 2024 announcement](https://www.mastercard.com/us/en/news-and-trends/press/2024/february/mastercard-supercharges-consumer-protection-with-gen-ai.html)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are adversarial drift, encrypted or missing telemetry, false-positive overload, analyst workflow gaps, and unclear containment authority. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Classification: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [FBI IC3 2024 Internet Crime Report](https://www.ic3.gov/AnnualReport/Reports/2024_ic3report.pdf); [IBM 2024 Cost of a Data Breach press release](https://newsroom.ibm.com/2024-07-30-ibm-report-escalating-data-breach-disruption-pushes-costs-to-new-highs); [Mastercard Decision Intelligence Pro 2024 announcement](https://www.mastercard.com/us/en/news-and-trends/press/2024/february/mastercard-supercharges-consumer-protection-with-gen-ai.html); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Vulnerability prioritization

1. **Name + aliases** — Vulnerability prioritization; aliases include risk-based vulnerability management, exploit prediction.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which vulnerabilities should be fixed first given exploitability and asset criticality. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: security. Outcome owner: CISO, SOC, vulnerability management, IAM, threat detection and response.
4. **Economic pool** — Cybersecurity value is loss avoidance and resilience: FBI IC3 recorded $16.6 billion in reported 2024 losses, and IBM placed the 2024 global average data-breach cost at $4.88 million. 5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **CVE/asset/package**. Decision: **patch, mitigate, accept, isolate**. Label/outcome: **exposure, SLA, exploited vulns**. Horizon: **daily/weekly**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from thousands to hundreds of thousands of daily or weekly decisions; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: SIEM, XDR/EDR, IAM, email security, vulnerability scanners, asset inventory, network telemetry, case management. In practice this data often exists but needs cleaning; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.

Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are exposure, SLA, exploited vulns, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.

Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [IBM 2024 Cost of a Data Breach press release](https://newsroom.ibm.com/2024-07-30-ibm-report-escalating-data-breach-disruption-pushes-costs-to-new-highs); [Mastercard Decision Intelligence Pro 2024 announcement](https://www.mastercard.com/us/en/news-and-trends/press/2024/february/mastercard-supercharges-consumer-protection-with-gen-ai.html)

## I. HR / people

### Attrition prediction

1. **Name + aliases** — Attrition prediction; aliases include turnover prediction, regretted-loss risk, flight risk.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which employees or cohorts are likely to leave and why. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: HR. Outcome owner: CHRO, talent acquisition, workforce planning, people analytics, HR business partners.
4. **Economic pool** — People risk is expensive: Gallup estimated U.S. businesses lose $1 trillion annually to voluntary turnover and that replacement can cost one-half to two times an employee’s annual salary. [Gallup turnover-cost analysis](https://www.gallup.com/workplace/247391/fixable-problem-costs-businesses-trillion.aspx)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **employee/team/role**. Decision: **manager intervention, comp review, career path, retention plan**. Label/outcome: **voluntary attrition, regretted loss**. Horizon: **monthly/quarterly**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from thousands to hundreds of thousands of daily or weekly decisions; value concentrates in large portfolios, shared services, or high-cost workflows.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: HRIS, ATS, LMS, workforce management, engagement surveys, performance, compensation, collaboration metadata. In practice this data exists unevenly and usually needs standardization; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to manager intervention, comp review, career path, retention plan; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are voluntary attrition, regretted loss, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium: labels, causality, process standardization, or integration are often the bottleneck. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Hourly-workforce settings emphasize scheduling and absence; knowledge-work settings emphasize skills, mobility, and retention; regulated jurisdictions require fairness and privacy governance.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to finance planning and operations scheduling; HR owns workforce supply, talent, retention, and fairness outcomes.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in pilots but less consistently scaled: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: public quantified enterprise examples are thinner; the economic benchmark is Gallup’s $1 trillion voluntary-turnover loss estimate, and BCG/MIT found only 10% of firms obtained significant AI financial benefits without organizational learning. [Gallup turnover-cost analysis](https://www.gallup.com/workplace/247391/fixable-problem-costs-businesses-trillion.aspx); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are biased labels, privacy and employee-trust concerns, manager inaction, proxy discrimination, and measuring prediction accuracy instead of retention or mobility outcomes. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Classification: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [Gallup turnover-cost analysis](https://www.gallup.com/workplace/247391/fixable-problem-costs-businesses-trillion.aspx); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are biased labels, privacy and employee-trust concerns, manager inaction, proxy discrimination, and measuring prediction accuracy instead of retention or mobility outcomes. ### Recruiting funnel optimization

1. **Name + aliases** — Recruiting funnel optimization; aliases include candidate conversion, hiring funnel analytics.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which recruiting steps, sources, or candidates create bottlenecks or high-quality hires. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: HR. Outcome owner: CHRO, talent acquisition, workforce planning, people analytics, HR business partners.
4. **Economic pool** — People risk is expensive: Gallup estimated U.S. businesses lose $1 trillion annually to voluntary turnover and that replacement can cost one-half to two times an employee’s annual salary. [Gallup turnover-cost analysis](https://www.gallup.com/workplace/247391/fixable-problem-costs-businesses-trillion.aspx)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **candidate/requisition/source/stage**. Decision: **source, nudge, prioritize, redesign funnel**. Label/outcome: **time-to-fill, quality-of-hire, conversion**. Horizon: **daily/weekly**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from thousands to hundreds of thousands of daily or weekly decisions; value concentrates in large portfolios, shared services, or high-cost workflows.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: HRIS, ATS, LMS, workforce management, engagement surveys, performance, compensation, collaboration metadata. In practice this data often exists but needs cleaning; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to source, nudge, prioritize, redesign funnel; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are time-to-fill, quality-of-hire, conversion, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium-high: data usually exists but requires integration, standard definitions, and human-in-the-loop workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Hourly-workforce settings emphasize scheduling and absence; knowledge-work settings emphasize skills, mobility, and retention; regulated jurisdictions require fairness and privacy governance.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to finance planning and operations scheduling; HR owns workforce supply, talent, retention, and fairness outcomes.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in scaled programs and advanced pilots: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: public quantified enterprise examples are thinner; the economic benchmark is Gallup’s $1 trillion voluntary-turnover loss estimate, and BCG/MIT found only 10% of firms obtained significant AI financial benefits without organizational learning. [Gallup turnover-cost analysis](https://www.gallup.com/workplace/247391/fixable-problem-costs-businesses-trillion.aspx); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are biased labels, privacy and employee-trust concerns, manager inaction, proxy discrimination, and measuring prediction accuracy instead of retention or mobility outcomes. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Scoring: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [Gallup turnover-cost analysis](https://www.gallup.com/workplace/247391/fixable-problem-costs-businesses-trillion.aspx); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Resume / job matching

1. **Name + aliases** — Resume / job matching; aliases include candidate matching, talent matching, semantic matching.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which candidates fit which jobs or projects based on skills, experience, and requirements. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: HR. Outcome owner: CHRO, talent acquisition, workforce planning, people analytics, HR business partners.
4. **Economic pool** — People risk is expensive: Gallup estimated U.S. businesses lose $1 trillion annually to voluntary turnover and that replacement can cost one-half to two times an employee’s annual salary. [Gallup turnover-cost analysis](https://www.gallup.com/workplace/247391/fixable-problem-costs-businesses-trillion.aspx)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **candidate/job/skill**. Decision: **rank, shortlist, route, recommend**. Label/outcome: **match quality, time-to-hire, diversity metrics**. Horizon: **per requisition**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates in large portfolios, shared services, or high-cost workflows.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: HRIS, ATS, LMS, workforce management, engagement surveys, performance, compensation, collaboration metadata. In practice this data exists unevenly and usually needs standardization; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to rank, shortlist, route, recommend; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are match quality, time-to-hire, diversity metrics, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium: labels, causality, process standardization, or integration are often the bottleneck. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. 12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to finance planning and operations scheduling; HR owns workforce supply, talent, retention, and fairness outcomes.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in pilots but less consistently scaled: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: public quantified enterprise examples are thinner; the economic benchmark is Gallup’s $1 trillion voluntary-turnover loss estimate, and BCG/MIT found only 10% of firms obtained significant AI financial benefits without organizational learning. [Gallup turnover-cost analysis](https://www.gallup.com/workplace/247391/fixable-problem-costs-businesses-trillion.aspx); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are biased labels, privacy and employee-trust concerns, manager inaction, proxy discrimination, and measuring prediction accuracy instead of retention or mobility outcomes. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)

### Scheduling optimization

1. **Name + aliases** — Scheduling optimization; aliases include shift scheduling, labor scheduling, roster optimization.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which employees should work which shifts given demand, preferences, rules, and cost. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: HR. Outcome owner: CHRO, talent acquisition, workforce planning, people analytics, HR business partners.
4. **Economic pool** — People risk is expensive: Gallup estimated U.S. businesses lose $1 trillion annually to voluntary turnover and that replacement can cost one-half to two times an employee’s annual salary. [Gallup turnover-cost analysis](https://www.gallup.com/workplace/247391/fixable-problem-costs-businesses-trillion.aspx)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **employee/shift/location**. Decision: **assign, swap, approve, staff**. Label/outcome: **labor cost, coverage, overtime, absence**. Horizon: **daily/weekly**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates in large portfolios, shared services, or high-cost workflows.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: HRIS, ATS, LMS, workforce management, engagement surveys, performance, compensation, collaboration metadata. In practice this data commonly exists in usable form; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to assign, swap, approve, staff; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are labor cost, coverage, overtime, absence, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — High: data and labels are usually captured in operational systems, and the action can be wired into existing workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)

12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to finance planning and operations scheduling; HR owns workforce supply, talent, retention, and fairness outcomes.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is deployed at scale in many data-mature enterprises: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: public quantified enterprise examples are thinner; the economic benchmark is Gallup’s $1 trillion voluntary-turnover loss estimate, and BCG/MIT found only 10% of firms obtained significant AI financial benefits without organizational learning. [Gallup turnover-cost analysis](https://www.gallup.com/workplace/247391/fixable-problem-costs-businesses-trillion.aspx); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are biased labels, privacy and employee-trust concerns, manager inaction, proxy discrimination, and measuring prediction accuracy instead of retention or mobility outcomes. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
