Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are mobility, fill rate, retention, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium: labels, causality, process standardization, or integration are often the bottleneck. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Hourly-workforce settings emphasize scheduling and absence; knowledge-work settings emphasize skills, mobility, and retention; regulated jurisdictions require fairness and privacy governance.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to finance planning and operations scheduling; HR owns workforce supply, talent, retention, and fairness outcomes.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in pilots but less consistently scaled: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: public quantified enterprise examples are thinner; the economic benchmark is Gallup’s $1 trillion voluntary-turnover loss estimate, and BCG/MIT found only 10% of firms obtained significant AI financial benefits without organizational learning. [Gallup turnover-cost analysis](https://www.gallup.com/workplace/247391/fixable-problem-costs-businesses-trillion.aspx); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are biased labels, privacy and employee-trust concerns, manager inaction, proxy discrimination, and measuring prediction accuracy instead of retention or mobility outcomes. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Recommendation: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [Gallup turnover-cost analysis](https://www.gallup.com/workplace/247391/fixable-problem-costs-businesses-trillion.aspx); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

## J. Legal & document-heavy work

### Contract review & extraction

1. **Name + aliases** — Contract review & extraction; aliases include contract analytics, clause extraction, CLM AI.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which terms, obligations, dates, risks, and deviations are present in contracts. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: legal. Outcome owner: general counsel, legal operations, contracting, compliance, records, discovery counsel.
4. **Economic pool** — Contract and document value is leakage and labor: KPMG reported more than 9% average contract-value leakage and 4.2% of contract value spent on avoidable post-award management. [KPMG contracting-process study](https://assets.kpmg.com/content/dam/kpmg/dk/pdf/Acor/2021/07/can-the-contracting-process-improve-without-an-owner.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **contract/clause/obligation**. Decision: **redline, approve, route, track**. Label/outcome: **cycle time, leakage, risk exceptions**. Horizon: **per contract**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: CLM, document management, e-discovery, email, DMS, policy systems, matter management, regulatory feeds. In practice this data often exists but needs cleaning; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to redline, approve, route, track; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are cycle time, leakage, risk exceptions, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium-high: data usually exists but requires integration, standard definitions, and human-in-the-loop workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — High-volume contracting emphasizes extraction and playbooks; litigation emphasizes recall and privilege; regulated operations emphasize evidence, audit trail, and defensible workflow.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to finance, procurement, and compliance; legal/document use cases own interpretation, obligation, privilege, and defensibility.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in scaled programs and advanced pilots: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: JPMorgan’s COiN was reported to save 360,000 hours of annual legal and loan-agreement review work; KPMG found contracting value leakage above 9%, creating a measurable target pool. [ABA Journal JPMorgan COiN report](https://www.abajournal.com/news/article/jpmorgan_chase_uses_tech_to_save_360000_hours_of_annual_work_by_lawyers_and); [KPMG contracting-process study](https://assets.kpmg.com/content/dam/kpmg/dk/pdf/Acor/2021/07/can-the-contracting-process-improve-without-an-owner.pdf)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are poor OCR, clause ambiguity, privilege risk, nonstandard templates, missing human review, and inability to prove defensible process. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Extraction: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [KPMG contracting-process study](https://assets.kpmg.com/content/dam/kpmg/dk/pdf/Acor/2021/07/can-the-contracting-process-improve-without-an-owner.pdf); [ABA Journal JPMorgan COiN report](https://www.abajournal.com/news/article/jpmorgan_chase_uses_tech_to_save_360000_hours_of_annual_work_by_lawyers_and); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Document classification & routing

1. **Name + aliases** — Document classification & routing; aliases include intelligent document processing, IDP, mailroom automation.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is what a document is and which business process should handle it. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: operations. Outcome owner: CFO, controller, treasurer, FP&A, credit, AP/AR, or shared-services leader.
4. **Economic pool** — Finance automation value comes from working capital, payment risk, and labor: PwC estimated €1.56 trillion of global excess working capital in 2024/25, and AFP found 79% of organizations experienced actual or attempted payments fraud in 2024. [PwC Working Capital Study 2024/25](https://www.pwc.co.uk/services/value-creation/insights/working-capital-study/2024.html); [2025 AFP Payments Fraud and Control Survey](https://www.truist.com/content/dam/truist-bank/us/en/documents/info/cci/2025-afp-payments-fraud-control-survey-report-key-highlights.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **document/email/form**. Decision: **classify, extract, route, archive**. Label/outcome: **cycle time, touchless rate, errors**. Horizon: **daily**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates in large portfolios, shared services, or high-cost workflows.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: ERP, AP/AR, treasury, TMS, bank statements, expense management, GL, close/reconciliation tools, procurement systems. In practice this data commonly exists in usable form; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to classify, extract, route, archive; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are cycle time, touchless rate, errors, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — High: data and labels are usually captured in operational systems, and the action can be wired into existing workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Large enterprises have ERP/AP/AR history; banks and insurers add regulated model governance; smaller firms often lack clean labels and standardized workflows.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to procurement, risk, and operations; finance use cases own cash, controls, close, and financial planning outcomes.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is deployed at scale in many data-mature enterprises: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: Genpact reported an enterprise AP program reaching 80% touchless processing, while HighRadius reported Staples reduced bad debt by 20% using collections automation. [Genpact 80% touchless AP case study](https://www.genpact.com/case-studies/from-manual-to-80-percent-touchless-accounts-payable-at-enterprise-scale); [HighRadius Staples collections case study](https://www.highradius.com/resources/Radiance/20-reduction-in-bad-debt-staples-collections-automation-journey-with-ai/)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are inconsistent master data, unclear approval authority, exception-heavy processes, audit concerns, and automating broken controls; payment-fraud prevalence and working-capital scale make false positives and control design material. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Classification: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [PwC Working Capital Study 2024/25](https://www.pwc.co.uk/services/value-creation/insights/working-capital-study/2024.html); [2025 AFP Payments Fraud and Control Survey](https://www.truist.com/content/dam/truist-bank/us/en/documents/info/cci/2025-afp-payments-fraud-control-survey-report-key-highlights.pdf); [Genpact 80% touchless AP case study](https://www.genpact.com/case-studies/from-manual-to-80-percent-touchless-accounts-payable-at-enterprise-scale); [HighRadius Staples collections case study](https://www.highradius.com/resources/Radiance/20-reduction-in-bad-debt-staples-collections-automation-journey-with-ai/); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Regulatory-change monitoring

1. **Name + aliases** — Regulatory-change monitoring; aliases include horizon scanning, regulatory intelligence.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which regulatory changes affect the enterprise and what policies or controls must change. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: legal. Outcome owner: general counsel, legal operations, contracting, compliance, records, discovery counsel.
4. **Economic pool** — Contract and document value is leakage and labor: KPMG reported more than 9% average contract-value leakage and 4.2% of contract value spent on avoidable post-award management. [KPMG contracting-process study](https://assets.kpmg.com/content/dam/kpmg/dk/pdf/Acor/2021/07/can-the-contracting-process-improve-without-an-owner.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **rule/regulation/control/business unit**. Decision: **assess, assign owner, update control, file**. Label/outcome: **compliance findings, response time**. Horizon: **weekly/monthly**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from hundreds to thousands of recurring decisions; value concentrates in large portfolios, shared services, or high-cost workflows.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: CLM, document management, e-discovery, email, DMS, policy systems, matter management, regulatory feeds. In practice this data is fragmented or sparse; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to assess, assign owner, update control, file; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are compliance findings, response time, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Low-medium: external data, sparse labels, or organizational ambiguity often dominate technical difficulty. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — High-volume contracting emphasizes extraction and playbooks; litigation emphasizes recall and privilege; regulated operations emphasize evidence, audit trail, and defensible workflow.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to finance, procurement, and compliance; legal/document use cases own interpretation, obligation, privilege, and defensibility.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is mostly exploratory: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: JPMorgan’s COiN was reported to save 360,000 hours of annual legal and loan-agreement review work; KPMG found contracting value leakage above 9%, creating a measurable target pool. [ABA Journal JPMorgan COiN report](https://www.abajournal.com/news/article/jpmorgan_chase_uses_tech_to_save_360000_hours_of_annual_work_by_lawyers_and); [KPMG contracting-process study](https://assets.kpmg.com/content/dam/kpmg/dk/pdf/Acor/2021/07/can-the-contracting-process-improve-without-an-owner.pdf)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are poor OCR, clause ambiguity, privilege risk, nonstandard templates, missing human review, and inability to prove defensible process. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — NLP: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [KPMG contracting-process study](https://assets.kpmg.com/content/dam/kpmg/dk/pdf/Acor/2021/07/can-the-contracting-process-improve-without-an-owner.pdf); [ABA Journal JPMorgan COiN report](https://www.abajournal.com/news/article/jpmorgan_chase_uses_tech_to_save_360000_hours_of_annual_work_by_lawyers_and); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Document summarization into structured records

1. **Name + aliases** — Document summarization into structured records; aliases include case summarization, note generation, structured extraction.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is how to convert long documents or conversations into accurate structured business records. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: legal. Outcome owner: general counsel, legal operations, contracting, compliance, records, discovery counsel.
4. **Economic pool** — Contract and document value is leakage and labor: KPMG reported more than 9% average contract-value leakage and 4.2% of contract value spent on avoidable post-award management. [KPMG contracting-process study](https://assets.kpmg.com/content/dam/kpmg/dk/pdf/Acor/2021/07/can-the-contracting-process-improve-without-an-owner.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **document/transcript/form**. Decision: **summarize, extract fields, file record**. Label/outcome: **cycle time, completeness, auditability**. Horizon: **per document**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates in large portfolios, shared services, or high-cost workflows.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: CLM, document management, e-discovery, email, DMS, policy systems, matter management, regulatory feeds. In practice this data exists unevenly and usually needs standardization; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to summarize, extract fields, file record; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are cycle time, completeness, auditability, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium: labels, causality, process standardization, or integration are often the bottleneck. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — High-volume contracting emphasizes extraction and playbooks; litigation emphasizes recall and privilege; regulated operations emphasize evidence, audit trail, and defensible workflow.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to finance, procurement, and compliance; legal/document use cases own interpretation, obligation, privilege, and defensibility.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in pilots but less consistently scaled: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: JPMorgan’s COiN was reported to save 360,000 hours of annual legal and loan-agreement review work; KPMG found contracting value leakage above 9%, creating a measurable target pool. [ABA Journal JPMorgan COiN report](https://www.abajournal.com/news/article/jpmorgan_chase_uses_tech_to_save_360000_hours_of_annual_work_by_lawyers_and); [KPMG contracting-process study](https://assets.kpmg.com/content/dam/kpmg/dk/pdf/Acor/2021/07/can-the-contracting-process-improve-without-an-owner.pdf)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are poor OCR, clause ambiguity, privilege risk, nonstandard templates, missing human review, and inability to prove defensible process. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Generative: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [KPMG contracting-process study](https://assets.kpmg.com/content/dam/kpmg/dk/pdf/Acor/2021/07/can-the-contracting-process-improve-without-an-owner.pdf); [ABA Journal JPMorgan COiN report](https://www.abajournal.com/news/article/jpmorgan_chase_uses_tech_to_save_360000_hours_of_annual_work_by_lawyers_and); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

## K. Executive / cross-functional

### KPI anomaly detection & alerting

1. **Name + aliases** — KPI anomaly detection & alerting; aliases include metric monitoring, business anomaly detection.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which business metrics have moved abnormally and need attention. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: executive. Outcome owner: CEO, COO, CFO, strategy, transformation office, corporate development, chief of staff.
4. **Economic pool** — Cross-functional analytics value is the management operating system: McKinsey estimated genAI could add $2.6 trillion to $4.4 trillion annually across 63 use cases, with roughly 75% of value in customer operations, marketing and sales, software engineering, and R&D. [McKinsey 2023 generative AI economic-potential report](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/tech-and-ai/our-insights/the-economic-potential-of-generative-ai-the-next-productivity-frontier)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **metric/business unit/time**. Decision: **alert, investigate, assign owner**. Label/outcome: **time-to-detect, false alerts, loss avoided**. Horizon: **daily/weekly**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates in large portfolios, shared services, or high-cost workflows.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: data warehouse/lakehouse, BI, planning, ERP, CRM, HRIS, external market/news data, board-reporting systems. In practice this data often exists but needs cleaning; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to alert, investigate, assign owner; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are time-to-detect, false alerts, loss avoided, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium-high: data usually exists but requires integration, standard definitions, and human-in-the-loop workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Executive analytics cuts across functions; the material variant is whether it is used for real operating decisions or only static reporting.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to every function; cross-functional use cases synthesize decision evidence rather than own frontline execution.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in scaled programs and advanced pilots: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: BCG found only 26% of companies had moved beyond proofs of concept to tangible value in 2024, and future-built companies in 2025 achieved five times the revenue increases and three times the cost reductions of others. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [BCG 2025 AI value-gap study](https://www.bcg.com/publications/2025/are-you-generating-value-from-ai-the-widening-gap)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are dashboards without owners, metric definitions that differ by function, hallucinated narrative, lack of drill-through, and executive decisions not tied to operating actions. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Anomaly: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [McKinsey 2023 generative AI economic-potential report](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/tech-and-ai/our-insights/the-economic-potential-of-generative-ai-the-next-productivity-frontier); [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [BCG 2025 AI value-gap study](https://www.bcg.com/publications/2025/are-you-generating-value-from-ai-the-widening-gap); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is why a metric moved and which drivers explain the change. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: executive. Outcome owner: CEO, COO, CFO, strategy, transformation office, corporate development, chief of staff.

### Board / ops reporting automation

1. **Name + aliases** — Board / ops reporting automation; aliases include management reporting, narrative generation, flash reporting.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is how to assemble, explain, and distribute accurate management reports quickly. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: executive. Outcome owner: CEO, COO, CFO, strategy, transformation office, corporate development, chief of staff.
4. **Economic pool** — Cross-functional analytics value is the management operating system: McKinsey estimated genAI could add $2.6 trillion to $4.4 trillion annually across 63 use cases, with roughly 75% of value in customer operations, marketing and sales, software engineering, and R&D. [McKinsey 2023 generative AI economic-potential report](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/tech-and-ai/our-insights/the-economic-potential-of-generative-ai-the-next-productivity-frontier)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **metric/report/business unit**. Decision: **generate report, request commentary, escalate**. Label/outcome: **cycle time, accuracy, follow-up actions**. Horizon: **weekly/monthly/quarterly**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates where senior decision latency or scarce expertise matters.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: data warehouse/lakehouse, BI, planning, ERP, CRM, HRIS, external market/news data, board-reporting systems. In practice this data exists unevenly and usually needs standardization; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to generate report, request commentary, escalate; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are cycle time, accuracy, follow-up actions, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium: labels, causality, process standardization, or integration are often the bottleneck. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Executive analytics cuts across functions; the material variant is whether it is used for real operating decisions or only static reporting.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to every function; cross-functional use cases synthesize decision evidence rather than own frontline execution.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in pilots but less consistently scaled: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: BCG found only 26% of companies had moved beyond proofs of concept to tangible value in 2024, and future-built companies in 2025 achieved five times the revenue increases and three times the cost reductions of others. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [BCG 2025 AI value-gap study](https://www.bcg.com/publications/2025/are-you-generating-value-from-ai-the-widening-gap)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are dashboards without owners, metric definitions that differ by function, hallucinated narrative, lack of drill-through, and executive decisions not tied to operating actions. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Generative: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [McKinsey 2023 generative AI economic-potential report](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/tech-and-ai/our-insights/the-economic-potential-of-generative-ai-the-next-productivity-frontier); [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [BCG 2025 AI value-gap study](https://www.bcg.com/publications/2025/are-you-generating-value-from-ai-the-widening-gap); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### M&A target screening

2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which companies, assets, or markets fit acquisition strategy and merit diligence. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.

14. **Documented results** — Representative results: BCG found only 26% of companies had moved beyond proofs of concept to tangible value in 2024, and future-built companies in 2025 achieved five times the revenue increases and three times the cost reductions of others. ## L. Industry-specific families

### Healthcare prior authorization

1. **Name + aliases** — Healthcare prior authorization; aliases include PA automation, utilization-management review.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which care requests require review, approval, additional evidence, or denial. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: healthcare. Outcome owner: chief medical officer, revenue-cycle leader, utilization management, care management, access operations.
4. **Economic pool** — Healthcare administrative value is large: CAQH estimated $89 billion spent on tracked U.S. healthcare administrative transactions in 2023 and $18.3 billion in savings opportunity from fully electronic transactions. [CAQH 2023 Index Report](https://www.caqh.org/hubfs/43908627/drupal/2024-01/2023_CAQH_Index_Report.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **authorization request/member/provider/service**. Decision: **approve, pend, request info, deny, expedite**. Label/outcome: **turnaround, admin cost, access**. Horizon: **real time/daily**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from thousands to hundreds of thousands of daily or weekly decisions; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: EHR, claims, prior authorization, scheduling, revenue-cycle, care management, HIE, call center, provider directory. In practice this data often exists but needs cleaning; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to approve, pend, request info, deny, expedite; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are turnaround, admin cost, access, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium-high: data usually exists but requires integration, standard definitions, and human-in-the-loop workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Payers focus on utilization and claims economics; providers focus on access, documentation, revenue cycle, and outcomes; life sciences focuses on compliance and evidence generation.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to finance and operations; healthcare use cases must keep clinical, administrative, and reimbursement outcomes distinct.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in scaled programs and advanced pilots: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: Geisinger/HealthHelp reported 50% to 70% reduction in escalated prior-authorization clinical-review cases and 76% reduction in approvals requiring human review; MetroHealth reduced high-risk no-show appointments from 36.2% to 32.8%. [HealthHelp/Geisinger prior-authorization case study](https://www.healthhelp.com/wp-content/uploads/HH-Geisinger-Case-Study_Transforming-Prior-Authorization-with-AI.pdf); [MetroHealth no-show intervention study](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10150669/)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are bias, workflow nonadoption, alert fatigue, lack of prospective validation, reimbursement misalignment, and clinical-safety governance gaps. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Classification: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [CAQH 2023 Index Report](https://www.caqh.org/hubfs/43908627/drupal/2024-01/2023_CAQH_Index_Report.pdf); [HealthHelp/Geisinger prior-authorization case study](https://www.healthhelp.com/wp-content/uploads/HH-Geisinger-Case-Study_Transforming-Prior-Authorization-with-AI.pdf); [MetroHealth no-show intervention study](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10150669/); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Healthcare claims / denials

1. **Name + aliases** — Healthcare claims / denials; aliases include denials management, claim adjudication exception, appeal prioritization.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which claims will deny, underpay, or merit appeal. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: healthcare. Outcome owner: chief medical officer, revenue-cycle leader, utilization management, care management, access operations.
4. **Economic pool** — Healthcare administrative value is large: CAQH estimated $89 billion spent on tracked U.S. healthcare administrative transactions in 2023 and $18.3 billion in savings opportunity from fully electronic transactions. [CAQH 2023 Index Report](https://www.caqh.org/hubfs/43908627/drupal/2024-01/2023_CAQH_Index_Report.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **claim/encounter/denial**. Decision: **correct, appeal, recover, prevent**. Label/outcome: **denial rate, recovery, AR days**. Horizon: **daily**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: EHR, claims, prior authorization, scheduling, revenue-cycle, care management, HIE, call center, provider directory. In practice this data commonly exists in usable form; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to correct, appeal, recover, prevent; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are denial rate, recovery, AR days, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — High: data and labels are usually captured in operational systems, and the action can be wired into existing workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Payers focus on utilization and claims economics; providers focus on access, documentation, revenue cycle, and outcomes; life sciences focuses on compliance and evidence generation.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to finance and operations; healthcare use cases must keep clinical, administrative, and reimbursement outcomes distinct.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is deployed at scale in many data-mature enterprises: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: Geisinger/HealthHelp reported 50% to 70% reduction in escalated prior-authorization clinical-review cases and 76% reduction in approvals requiring human review; MetroHealth reduced high-risk no-show appointments from 36.2% to 32.8%. [HealthHelp/Geisinger prior-authorization case study](https://www.healthhelp.com/wp-content/uploads/HH-Geisinger-Case-Study_Transforming-Prior-Authorization-with-AI.pdf); [MetroHealth no-show intervention study](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10150669/)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are bias, workflow nonadoption, alert fatigue, lack of prospective validation, reimbursement misalignment, and clinical-safety governance gaps. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Scoring: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [CAQH 2023 Index Report](https://www.caqh.org/hubfs/43908627/drupal/2024-01/2023_CAQH_Index_Report.pdf); [HealthHelp/Geisinger prior-authorization case study](https://www.healthhelp.com/wp-content/uploads/HH-Geisinger-Case-Study_Transforming-Prior-Authorization-with-AI.pdf); [MetroHealth no-show intervention study](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10150669/); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Clinical documentation

1. **Name + aliases** — Clinical documentation; aliases include ambient documentation, clinical note generation, coding support.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is how to produce complete clinical notes and structured records from encounters. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: healthcare. Outcome owner: chief medical officer, revenue-cycle leader, utilization management, care management, access operations.
4. **Economic pool** — Healthcare administrative value is large: CAQH estimated $89 billion spent on tracked U.S. healthcare administrative transactions in 2023 and $18.3 billion in savings opportunity from fully electronic transactions. [CAQH 2023 Index Report](https://www.caqh.org/hubfs/43908627/drupal/2024-01/2023_CAQH_Index_Report.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **encounter/note/problem/code**. Decision: **draft note, code, query provider**. Label/outcome: **clinician time, coding quality, burnout**. Horizon: **per visit**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from thousands to hundreds of thousands of daily or weekly decisions; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: EHR, claims, prior authorization, scheduling, revenue-cycle, care management, HIE, call center, provider directory. In practice this data exists unevenly and usually needs standardization; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to draft note, code, query provider; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are clinician time, coding quality, burnout, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium: labels, causality, process standardization, or integration are often the bottleneck. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Payers focus on utilization and claims economics; providers focus on access, documentation, revenue cycle, and outcomes; life sciences focuses on compliance and evidence generation.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to finance and operations; healthcare use cases must keep clinical, administrative, and reimbursement outcomes distinct.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in pilots but less consistently scaled: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: Geisinger/HealthHelp reported 50% to 70% reduction in escalated prior-authorization clinical-review cases and 76% reduction in approvals requiring human review; MetroHealth reduced high-risk no-show appointments from 36.2% to 32.8%. [HealthHelp/Geisinger prior-authorization case study](https://www.healthhelp.com/wp-content/uploads/HH-Geisinger-Case-Study_Transforming-Prior-Authorization-with-AI.pdf); [MetroHealth no-show intervention study](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10150669/)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are bias, workflow nonadoption, alert fatigue, lack of prospective validation, reimbursement misalignment, and clinical-safety governance gaps. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Generative: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [CAQH 2023 Index Report](https://www.caqh.org/hubfs/43908627/drupal/2024-01/2023_CAQH_Index_Report.pdf); [HealthHelp/Geisinger prior-authorization case study](https://www.healthhelp.com/wp-content/uploads/HH-Geisinger-Case-Study_Transforming-Prior-Authorization-with-AI.pdf); [MetroHealth no-show intervention study](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10150669/); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Patient no-show

1. **Name + aliases** — Patient no-show; aliases include appointment no-show prediction, access optimization.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which scheduled patients are unlikely to attend and which intervention improves attendance. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: healthcare. Outcome owner: chief medical officer, revenue-cycle leader, utilization management, care management, access operations.
4. **Economic pool** — Healthcare administrative value is large: CAQH estimated $89 billion spent on tracked U.S. healthcare administrative transactions in 2023 and $18.3 billion in savings opportunity from fully electronic transactions. [CAQH 2023 Index Report](https://www.caqh.org/hubfs/43908627/drupal/2024-01/2023_CAQH_Index_Report.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **appointment/patient/clinic**. Decision: **remind, overbook, reschedule, call**. Label/outcome: **no-show rate, access, utilization**. Horizon: **daily**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates in large portfolios, shared services, or high-cost workflows.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: EHR, claims, prior authorization, scheduling, revenue-cycle, care management, HIE, call center, provider directory. In practice this data commonly exists in usable form; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to remind, overbook, reschedule, call; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are no-show rate, access, utilization, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — High: data and labels are usually captured in operational systems, and the action can be wired into existing workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Payers focus on utilization and claims economics; providers focus on access, documentation, revenue cycle, and outcomes; life sciences focuses on compliance and evidence generation.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to finance and operations; healthcare use cases must keep clinical, administrative, and reimbursement outcomes distinct.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is deployed at scale in many data-mature enterprises: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: Geisinger/HealthHelp reported 50% to 70% reduction in escalated prior-authorization clinical-review cases and 76% reduction in approvals requiring human review; MetroHealth reduced high-risk no-show appointments from 36.2% to 32.8%. [HealthHelp/Geisinger prior-authorization case study](https://www.healthhelp.com/wp-content/uploads/HH-Geisinger-Case-Study_Transforming-Prior-Authorization-with-AI.pdf); [MetroHealth no-show intervention study](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10150669/)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are bias, workflow nonadoption, alert fatigue, lack of prospective validation, reimbursement misalignment, and clinical-safety governance gaps. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Classification: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [CAQH 2023 Index Report](https://www.caqh.org/hubfs/43908627/drupal/2024-01/2023_CAQH_Index_Report.pdf); [HealthHelp/Geisinger prior-authorization case study](https://www.healthhelp.com/wp-content/uploads/HH-Geisinger-Case-Study_Transforming-Prior-Authorization-with-AI.pdf); [MetroHealth no-show intervention study](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10150669/); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Readmission risk

1. **Name + aliases** — Readmission risk; aliases include 30-day readmission prediction, transition-of-care risk.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which discharged patients are likely to be readmitted. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: healthcare. Outcome owner: chief medical officer, quality leader, care transitions, population health, case management.
4. **Economic pool** — Readmission risk has reimbursement and care-quality value because CMS reduces payments for excess 30-day readmissions, with a statutory cap of 3% of payments under the HRRP. [CMS Hospital Readmissions Reduction Program](https://www.cms.gov/medicare/payment/prospective-payment-systems/acute-inpatient-pps/hospital-readmissions-reduction-program-hrrp)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **patient/discharge/episode**. Decision: **care transition, outreach, home health, medication review**. Label/outcome: **30-day readmission, cost, quality**. Horizon: **daily**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from thousands to hundreds of thousands of daily or weekly decisions; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: EHR, ADT, discharge summaries, claims, care-management systems, pharmacy, social determinants, home-health records. In practice this data often exists but needs cleaning; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to care transition, outreach, home health, medication review; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are 30-day readmission, cost, quality, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium-high: data usually exists but requires integration, standard definitions, and human-in-the-loop workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Hospitals use episode and discharge data; payers use claims and care-management signals; post-acute providers use care-transition and social-risk signals.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to care-gap and no-show; readmission is post-discharge acute utilization, not appointment adherence or preventive-care completion.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in scaled programs and advanced pilots: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: BCG found only 26% of companies had moved beyond proofs of concept to tangible value in 2024, and future-built companies in 2025 achieved five times the revenue increases and three times the cost reductions of others. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [BCG 2025 AI value-gap study](https://www.bcg.com/publications/2025/are-you-generating-value-from-ai-the-widening-gap)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are socioeconomic confounding, intervention scarcity, shifting patient mix, and optimizing penalty metrics without improving care transitions. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Classification: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [CMS Hospital Readmissions Reduction Program](https://www.cms.gov/medicare/payment/prospective-payment-systems/acute-inpatient-pps/hospital-readmissions-reduction-program-hrrp); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Care-gap identification

1. **Name + aliases** — Care-gap identification; aliases include HEDIS gap closure, preventive-care outreach.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which patients are missing recommended care and what outreach should close the gap. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: healthcare. Outcome owner: chief medical officer, revenue-cycle leader, utilization management, care management, access operations.
4. **Economic pool** — Healthcare administrative value is large: CAQH estimated $89 billion spent on tracked U.S. healthcare administrative transactions in 2023 and $18.3 billion in savings opportunity from fully electronic transactions. [CAQH 2023 Index Report](https://www.caqh.org/hubfs/43908627/drupal/2024-01/2023_CAQH_Index_Report.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **member/patient/gap**. Decision: **outreach, schedule, remind, close gap**. Label/outcome: **gap closure, quality score, outcomes**. Horizon: **weekly/monthly**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates in large portfolios, shared services, or high-cost workflows.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: EHR, claims, prior authorization, scheduling, revenue-cycle, care management, HIE, call center, provider directory. In practice this data commonly exists in usable form; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to outreach, schedule, remind, close gap; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are gap closure, quality score, outcomes, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — High: data and labels are usually captured in operational systems, and the action can be wired into existing workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Payers focus on utilization and claims economics; providers focus on access, documentation, revenue cycle, and outcomes; life sciences focuses on compliance and evidence generation.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to finance and operations; healthcare use cases must keep clinical, administrative, and reimbursement outcomes distinct.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is deployed at scale in many data-mature enterprises: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: Geisinger/HealthHelp reported 50% to 70% reduction in escalated prior-authorization clinical-review cases and 76% reduction in approvals requiring human review; MetroHealth reduced high-risk no-show appointments from 36.2% to 32.8%. [HealthHelp/Geisinger prior-authorization case study](https://www.healthhelp.com/wp-content/uploads/HH-Geisinger-Case-Study_Transforming-Prior-Authorization-with-AI.pdf); [MetroHealth no-show intervention study](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10150669/)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are bias, workflow nonadoption, alert fatigue, lack of prospective validation, reimbursement misalignment, and clinical-safety governance gaps. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Rules+ML: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
