### Banking collections

1. **Name + aliases** — Banking collections; aliases include delinquency treatment, early-stage collections.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which delinquent borrowers should receive which treatment to maximize cure and minimize loss. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: banking. Outcome owner: CFO, controller, treasurer, FP&A, credit, AP/AR, or shared-services leader.
4. **Economic pool** — Finance automation value comes from working capital, payment risk, and labor: PwC estimated €1.56 trillion of global excess working capital in 2024/25, and AFP found 79% of organizations experienced actual or attempted payments fraud in 2024. [PwC Working Capital Study 2024/25](https://www.pwc.co.uk/services/value-creation/insights/working-capital-study/2024.html); [2025 AFP Payments Fraud and Control Survey](https://www.truist.com/content/dam/truist-bank/us/en/documents/info/cci/2025-afp-payments-fraud-control-survey-report-key-highlights.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **loan/account/customer**. Decision: **call, restructure, hardship, legal, write-off**. Label/outcome: **roll rate, cure, loss**. Horizon: **daily/monthly**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: ERP, AP/AR, treasury, TMS, bank statements, expense management, GL, close/reconciliation tools, procurement systems. In practice this data commonly exists in usable form; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to call, restructure, hardship, legal, write-off; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are roll rate, cure, loss, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — High: data and labels are usually captured in operational systems, and the action can be wired into existing workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Large enterprises have ERP/AP/AR history; banks and insurers add regulated model governance; smaller firms often lack clean labels and standardized workflows.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to procurement, risk, and operations; finance use cases own cash, controls, close, and financial planning outcomes.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is deployed at scale in many data-mature enterprises: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: Genpact reported an enterprise AP program reaching 80% touchless processing, while HighRadius reported Staples reduced bad debt by 20% using collections automation. [Genpact 80% touchless AP case study](https://www.genpact.com/case-studies/from-manual-to-80-percent-touchless-accounts-payable-at-enterprise-scale); [HighRadius Staples collections case study](https://www.highradius.com/resources/Radiance/20-reduction-in-bad-debt-staples-collections-automation-journey-with-ai/)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are inconsistent master data, unclear approval authority, exception-heavy processes, audit concerns, and automating broken controls; payment-fraud prevalence and working-capital scale make false positives and control design material. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Scoring: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [PwC Working Capital Study 2024/25](https://www.pwc.co.uk/services/value-creation/insights/working-capital-study/2024.html); [2025 AFP Payments Fraud and Control Survey](https://www.truist.com/content/dam/truist-bank/us/en/documents/info/cci/2025-afp-payments-fraud-control-survey-report-key-highlights.pdf); [Genpact 80% touchless AP case study](https://www.genpact.com/case-studies/from-manual-to-80-percent-touchless-accounts-payable-at-enterprise-scale); [HighRadius Staples collections case study](https://www.highradius.com/resources/Radiance/20-reduction-in-bad-debt-staples-collections-automation-journey-with-ai/); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Banking limit management

1. **Name + aliases** — Banking limit management; aliases include credit-line management, exposure optimization.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is what credit limit or exposure should be extended or reduced. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: banking. Outcome owner: CFO, controller, treasurer, FP&A, credit, AP/AR, or shared-services leader.
4. **Economic pool** — Finance automation value comes from working capital, payment risk, and labor: PwC estimated €1.56 trillion of global excess working capital in 2024/25, and AFP found 79% of organizations experienced actual or attempted payments fraud in 2024. [PwC Working Capital Study 2024/25](https://www.pwc.co.uk/services/value-creation/insights/working-capital-study/2024.html); [2025 AFP Payments Fraud and Control Survey](https://www.truist.com/content/dam/truist-bank/us/en/documents/info/cci/2025-afp-payments-fraud-control-survey-report-key-highlights.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **account/customer/portfolio**. Decision: **increase, decrease, freeze, price**. Label/outcome: **loss, utilization, revenue**. Horizon: **monthly/real time**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from thousands to hundreds of thousands of daily or weekly decisions; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: ERP, AP/AR, treasury, TMS, bank statements, expense management, GL, close/reconciliation tools, procurement systems. In practice this data often exists but needs cleaning; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to increase, decrease, freeze, price; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are loss, utilization, revenue, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium-high: data usually exists but requires integration, standard definitions, and human-in-the-loop workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Large enterprises have ERP/AP/AR history; banks and insurers add regulated model governance; smaller firms often lack clean labels and standardized workflows.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to procurement, risk, and operations; finance use cases own cash, controls, close, and financial planning outcomes.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in scaled programs and advanced pilots: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: Genpact reported an enterprise AP program reaching 80% touchless processing, while HighRadius reported Staples reduced bad debt by 20% using collections automation. [Genpact 80% touchless AP case study](https://www.genpact.com/case-studies/from-manual-to-80-percent-touchless-accounts-payable-at-enterprise-scale); [HighRadius Staples collections case study](https://www.highradius.com/resources/Radiance/20-reduction-in-bad-debt-staples-collections-automation-journey-with-ai/)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are inconsistent master data, unclear approval authority, exception-heavy processes, audit concerns, and automating broken controls; payment-fraud prevalence and working-capital scale make false positives and control design material. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Optimization: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [PwC Working Capital Study 2024/25](https://www.pwc.co.uk/services/value-creation/insights/working-capital-study/2024.html); [2025 AFP Payments Fraud and Control Survey](https://www.truist.com/content/dam/truist-bank/us/en/documents/info/cci/2025-afp-payments-fraud-control-survey-report-key-highlights.pdf); [Genpact 80% touchless AP case study](https://www.genpact.com/case-studies/from-manual-to-80-percent-touchless-accounts-payable-at-enterprise-scale); [HighRadius Staples collections case study](https://www.highradius.com/resources/Radiance/20-reduction-in-bad-debt-staples-collections-automation-journey-with-ai/); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Transaction categorization

2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is what category, merchant, and purpose a financial transaction represents. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: banking. Outcome owner: CFO, controller, treasurer, FP&A, credit, AP/AR, or shared-services leader.

6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates where senior decision latency or scarce expertise matters.

13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is deployed at scale in many data-mature enterprises: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. ### Insurance claims triage

1. **Name + aliases** — Insurance claims triage; aliases include FNOL triage, straight-through claims.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which claims can be paid automatically and which require adjuster, investigation, or special handling. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: insurance. Outcome owner: chief risk officer, fraud operations, trust and safety, payments risk, investigations.
4. **Economic pool** — Fraud is a direct loss pool: ACFE estimated organizations lose 5% of revenue to fraud annually, and CyberSource reported merchants estimate 3% of ecommerce revenue is lost to fraud each year. [ACFE 2024 Report to the Nations](https://www.acfe.com/-/media/files/acfe/pdfs/rttn/2024/2024-report-to-the-nations.pdf); [2024 Global eCommerce Payments & Fraud Report](https://www.cybersource.com/content/dam/documents/campaign/fraud-report/global-fraud-report-2024.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **claim/policy/loss**. Decision: **route, pay, inspect, investigate**. Label/outcome: **cycle time, leakage, CX**. Horizon: **daily**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: payment gateway, order management, device/session telemetry, chargeback systems, case management, identity/KYC, graph store. In practice this data commonly exists in usable form; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to route, pay, inspect, investigate; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are cycle time, leakage, CX, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — High: data and labels are usually captured in operational systems, and the action can be wired into existing workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Payments emphasize millisecond decisions and false-positive cost; insurance and public-sector fraud emphasize investigation yield; internal fraud emphasizes audit evidence and controls.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to cybersecurity and compliance; fraud use cases decide monetary approval, investigation, or recovery, whereas cyber use cases decide technical containment.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is deployed at scale in many data-mature enterprises: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: Mastercard reported average fraud-detection-rate improvement of 20%, up to 300% in some cases, and false-positive reduction of more than 85%; CyberSource reported ecommerce merchants use one to two AI/ML fraud tools on average. [Mastercard Decision Intelligence Pro 2024 announcement](https://www.mastercard.com/us/en/news-and-trends/press/2024/february/mastercard-supercharges-consumer-protection-with-gen-ai.html); [2024 Global eCommerce Payments & Fraud Report](https://www.cybersource.com/content/dam/documents/campaign/fraud-report/global-fraud-report-2024.pdf)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are adversarial adaptation, false positives that block good customers, delayed labels, investigation bottlenecks, and weak feedback from chargebacks or SAR outcomes. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Classification: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [ACFE 2024 Report to the Nations](https://www.acfe.com/-/media/files/acfe/pdfs/rttn/2024/2024-report-to-the-nations.pdf); [2024 Global eCommerce Payments & Fraud Report](https://www.cybersource.com/content/dam/documents/campaign/fraud-report/global-fraud-report-2024.pdf); [Mastercard Decision Intelligence Pro 2024 announcement](https://www.mastercard.com/us/en/news-and-trends/press/2024/february/mastercard-supercharges-consumer-protection-with-gen-ai.html); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Subrogation detection

1. **Name + aliases** — Subrogation detection; aliases include recovery opportunity detection, third-party liability.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which claims have recoverable third-party liability. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.

9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are recovery dollars, hit rate, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.

Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Payments emphasize millisecond decisions and false-positive cost; insurance and public-sector fraud emphasize investigation yield; internal fraud emphasizes audit evidence and controls.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to cybersecurity and compliance; fraud use cases decide monetary approval, investigation, or recovery, whereas cyber use cases decide technical containment.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in scaled programs and advanced pilots: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: Mastercard reported average fraud-detection-rate improvement of 20%, up to 300% in some cases, and false-positive reduction of more than 85%; CyberSource reported ecommerce merchants use one to two AI/ML fraud tools on average. [Mastercard Decision Intelligence Pro 2024 announcement](https://www.mastercard.com/us/en/news-and-trends/press/2024/february/mastercard-supercharges-consumer-protection-with-gen-ai.html); [2024 Global eCommerce Payments & Fraud Report](https://www.cybersource.com/content/dam/documents/campaign/fraud-report/global-fraud-report-2024.pdf)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are adversarial adaptation, false positives that block good customers, delayed labels, investigation bottlenecks, and weak feedback from chargebacks or SAR outcomes. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Classification: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [ACFE 2024 Report to the Nations](https://www.acfe.com/-/media/files/acfe/pdfs/rttn/2024/2024-report-to-the-nations.pdf); [2024 Global eCommerce Payments & Fraud Report](https://www.cybersource.com/content/dam/documents/campaign/fraud-report/global-fraud-report-2024.pdf); [Mastercard Decision Intelligence Pro 2024 announcement](https://www.mastercard.com/us/en/news-and-trends/press/2024/february/mastercard-supercharges-consumer-protection-with-gen-ai.html); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Retail assortment

1. **Name + aliases** — Retail assortment; aliases include assortment optimization, category assortment planning.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which products should be carried in which stores or digital placements. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: retail. Outcome owner: chief merchant, store operations, ecommerce, loss prevention, inventory planning.
4. **Economic pool** — Retail shrink and inventory value are material: NRF reported $112.1 billion in 2022 retail shrink losses, and IHL/Sensormatic estimated $1.77 trillion in 2023 global inventory distortion. [NRF National Retail Security Survey 2023](https://nrf.com/research/national-retail-security-survey-2023); [IHL/Sensormatic 2023 inventory distortion study](https://www.sensormatic.com/resources/rp/2023/ihl-inventory-distortion-study)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **SKU/store/category**. Decision: **add, delist, localize, substitute**. Label/outcome: **sales, margin, turns, availability**. Horizon: **season/monthly**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from thousands to hundreds of thousands of daily or weekly decisions; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: POS, ecommerce, OMS, WMS, planogram, loyalty, video/loss-prevention, inventory, pricing/promotion systems. In practice this data often exists but needs cleaning; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to add, delist, localize, substitute; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are sales, margin, turns, availability, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium-high: data usually exists but requires integration, standard definitions, and human-in-the-loop workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Grocery and fashion face high assortment and inventory volatility; specialty retail emphasizes recommendations and attach; stores add video, labor, and shrink signals.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to supply-chain and marketing; retail-specific use cases own store/SKU/customer physical and omnichannel outcomes.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in scaled programs and advanced pilots: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: Carrefour reported +21% LTV ROAS; DHL’s demand and inventory planning case reported +10% forecast accuracy and more than €4 million working-capital savings. [Google/Carrefour LTV case study](https://services.google.com/fh/files/blogs/carrefour_case_study.pdf); [DHL analytics case-study deck](https://www.dhl.com/content/dam/dhl/global/core/documents/pdf/glo-core-analytics-breakout.pdf)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are poor inventory accuracy, promotion confounding, seasonality, cold starts, store execution variability, and shrink false positives. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Optimization: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [NRF National Retail Security Survey 2023](https://nrf.com/research/national-retail-security-survey-2023); [IHL/Sensormatic 2023 inventory distortion study](https://www.sensormatic.com/resources/rp/2023/ihl-inventory-distortion-study); [Google/Carrefour LTV case study](https://services.google.com/fh/files/blogs/carrefour_case_study.pdf); [DHL analytics case-study deck](https://www.dhl.com/content/dam/dhl/global/core/documents/pdf/glo-core-analytics-breakout.pdf); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Telecom network optimization

1. **Name + aliases** — Telecom network optimization; aliases include RAN optimization, capacity optimization, self-optimizing network.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is where network capacity, interference, or energy settings should change. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: telecom. Outcome owner: grid operations, asset management, reliability engineering, field operations, regulatory operations.
4. **Economic pool** — Grid and utility value is interruption avoidance and asset efficiency; U.S. DOE/LBNL studies estimated U.S. power interruption costs at $79 billion annually in a base-case estimate. [U.S. DOE outage-cost study](https://www.energy.gov/oe/articles/understanding-cost-power-interruptions-us-electricity-consumers)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **cell/site/device/traffic hour**. Decision: **tune, add capacity, reroute, power save**. Label/outcome: **throughput, drops, energy, CX**. Horizon: **real time/hourly**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from thousands to hundreds of thousands of daily or weekly decisions; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: SCADA, AMI meters, GIS, outage-management, asset management, weather, DERMS, field-service systems. In practice this data often exists but needs cleaning; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to tune, add capacity, reroute, power save; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are throughput, drops, energy, CX, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium-high: data usually exists but requires integration, standard definitions, and human-in-the-loop workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Power, gas, water, and telecom networks all manage assets, load, outages, and field crews, but differ in safety constraints, telemetry density, and regulatory reporting.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to industrial maintenance and logistics; utilities add grid physics, regulated reliability, and public-safety constraints.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in scaled programs and advanced pilots: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: BCG found only 26% of companies had moved beyond proofs of concept to tangible value in 2024, and future-built companies in 2025 achieved five times the revenue increases and three times the cost reductions of others. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [BCG 2025 AI value-gap study](https://www.bcg.com/publications/2025/are-you-generating-value-from-ai-the-widening-gap)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are rare-event labels, safety-critical validation, telemetry gaps on aging assets, and regulatory caution around automated control. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Optimization: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [U.S. DOE outage-cost study](https://www.energy.gov/oe/articles/understanding-cost-power-interruptions-us-electricity-consumers); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Telecom field-service dispatch

1. **Name + aliases** — Telecom field-service dispatch; aliases include truck-roll optimization, technician dispatch, first-time-fix.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which technician should visit which job with which parts and route. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: telecom. Outcome owner: VP logistics, transportation, field service, fleet operations, fulfillment leader.
4. **Economic pool** — Logistics value appears in route cost, capacity, service reliability, and delay avoidance; DHL reported a demand-and-inventory analytics case with 23% storage-space reduction, +10% forecast-accuracy improvement, and more than €4 million working-capital savings. [DHL analytics case-study deck](https://www.dhl.com/content/dam/dhl/global/core/documents/pdf/glo-core-analytics-breakout.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **work order/technician/customer/device**. Decision: **dispatch, reschedule, preload parts**. Label/outcome: **first-time-fix, cost, ETA**. Horizon: **daily/real time**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates in large portfolios, shared services, or high-cost workflows.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: TMS, WMS, route planning, telematics, GPS, order management, customer notification, driver apps. In practice this data commonly exists in usable form; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to dispatch, reschedule, preload parts; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are first-time-fix, cost, ETA, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — High: data and labels are usually captured in operational systems, and the action can be wired into existing workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Parcel and food delivery decide in real time; freight and ocean logistics optimize multi-day networks; field service adds technician skill, parts, and customer-access constraints.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to order promising, customer notifications, and fleet maintenance; logistics owns movement, route, ETA, and utilization decisions.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is deployed at scale in many data-mature enterprises: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: BCG found only 26% of companies had moved beyond proofs of concept to tangible value in 2024, and future-built companies in 2025 achieved five times the revenue increases and three times the cost reductions of others. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [BCG 2025 AI value-gap study](https://www.bcg.com/publications/2025/are-you-generating-value-from-ai-the-widening-gap)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are stale traffic/constraint data, driver noncompliance, unrealistic promises, exception storms, and local optimization that hurts network cost. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Optimization: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [DHL analytics case-study deck](https://www.dhl.com/content/dam/dhl/global/core/documents/pdf/glo-core-analytics-breakout.pdf); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Energy load forecasting

1. **Name + aliases** — Energy load forecasting; aliases include electricity demand forecasting, net-load forecasting.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is how much load or generation will appear by node and time. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: energy. Outcome owner: grid operations, asset management, reliability engineering, field operations, regulatory operations.
4. **Economic pool** — Grid and utility value is interruption avoidance and asset efficiency; U.S. DOE/LBNL studies estimated U.S. power interruption costs at $79 billion annually in a base-case estimate. [U.S. DOE outage-cost study](https://www.energy.gov/oe/articles/understanding-cost-power-interruptions-us-electricity-consumers)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **meter/feeder/region/time**. Decision: **dispatch, procure, balance, price**. Label/outcome: **forecast error, imbalance, cost**. Horizon: **hourly/daily**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: SCADA, AMI meters, GIS, outage-management, asset management, weather, DERMS, field-service systems. In practice this data commonly exists in usable form; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to dispatch, procure, balance, price; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are forecast error, imbalance, cost, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — High: data and labels are usually captured in operational systems, and the action can be wired into existing workflow. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Power, gas, water, and telecom networks all manage assets, load, outages, and field crews, but differ in safety constraints, telemetry density, and regulatory reporting.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to industrial maintenance and logistics; utilities add grid physics, regulated reliability, and public-safety constraints.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is deployed at scale in many data-mature enterprises: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: BCG found only 26% of companies had moved beyond proofs of concept to tangible value in 2024, and future-built companies in 2025 achieved five times the revenue increases and three times the cost reductions of others. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [BCG 2025 AI value-gap study](https://www.bcg.com/publications/2025/are-you-generating-value-from-ai-the-widening-gap)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are rare-event labels, safety-critical validation, telemetry gaps on aging assets, and regulatory caution around automated control. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Forecasting: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [U.S. DOE outage-cost study](https://www.energy.gov/oe/articles/understanding-cost-power-interruptions-us-electricity-consumers); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Public benefits fraud

1. **Name + aliases** — Public benefits fraud; aliases include program-integrity analytics, improper-payment detection.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which claims, applications, or providers indicate fraud, waste, or abuse. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: public sector. Outcome owner: chief risk officer, fraud operations, trust and safety, payments risk, investigations.
4. **Economic pool** — Fraud is a direct loss pool: ACFE estimated organizations lose 5% of revenue to fraud annually, and CyberSource reported merchants estimate 3% of ecommerce revenue is lost to fraud each year. [ACFE 2024 Report to the Nations](https://www.acfe.com/-/media/files/acfe/pdfs/rttn/2024/2024-report-to-the-nations.pdf); [2024 Global eCommerce Payments & Fraud Report](https://www.cybersource.com/content/dam/documents/campaign/fraud-report/global-fraud-report-2024.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **benefit claim/person/provider**. Decision: **review, suspend, investigate, recover**. Label/outcome: **improper payments, fairness, backlog**. Horizon: **daily/monthly**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from hundreds to thousands of recurring decisions; value concentrates in large portfolios, shared services, or high-cost workflows.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: payment gateway, order management, device/session telemetry, chargeback systems, case management, identity/KYC, graph store. In practice this data is fragmented or sparse; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to review, suspend, investigate, recover; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are improper payments, fairness, backlog, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Low-medium: external data, sparse labels, or organizational ambiguity often dominate technical difficulty. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Payments emphasize millisecond decisions and false-positive cost; insurance and public-sector fraud emphasize investigation yield; internal fraud emphasizes audit evidence and controls.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to cybersecurity and compliance; fraud use cases decide monetary approval, investigation, or recovery, whereas cyber use cases decide technical containment.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is mostly exploratory: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: Mastercard reported average fraud-detection-rate improvement of 20%, up to 300% in some cases, and false-positive reduction of more than 85%; CyberSource reported ecommerce merchants use one to two AI/ML fraud tools on average. [Mastercard Decision Intelligence Pro 2024 announcement](https://www.mastercard.com/us/en/news-and-trends/press/2024/february/mastercard-supercharges-consumer-protection-with-gen-ai.html); [2024 Global eCommerce Payments & Fraud Report](https://www.cybersource.com/content/dam/documents/campaign/fraud-report/global-fraud-report-2024.pdf)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are adversarial adaptation, false positives that block good customers, delayed labels, investigation bottlenecks, and weak feedback from chargebacks or SAR outcomes. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Anomaly: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [ACFE 2024 Report to the Nations](https://www.acfe.com/-/media/files/acfe/pdfs/rttn/2024/2024-report-to-the-nations.pdf); [2024 Global eCommerce Payments & Fraud Report](https://www.cybersource.com/content/dam/documents/campaign/fraud-report/global-fraud-report-2024.pdf); [Mastercard Decision Intelligence Pro 2024 announcement](https://www.mastercard.com/us/en/news-and-trends/press/2024/february/mastercard-supercharges-consumer-protection-with-gen-ai.html); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Public case prioritization

1. **Name + aliases** — Public case prioritization; aliases include case triage, risk-based inspections, service prioritization.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which public cases need urgent attention or scarce worker capacity. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: public sector. Outcome owner: CEO, COO, CFO, strategy, transformation office, corporate development, chief of staff.
4. **Economic pool** — Cross-functional analytics value is the management operating system: McKinsey estimated genAI could add $2.6 trillion to $4.4 trillion annually across 63 use cases, with roughly 75% of value in customer operations, marketing and sales, software engineering, and R&D. [McKinsey 2023 generative AI economic-potential report](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/tech-and-ai/our-insights/the-economic-potential-of-generative-ai-the-next-productivity-frontier)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **case/person/location**. Decision: **prioritize, assign, inspect, refer**. Label/outcome: **outcomes, backlog, timeliness**. Horizon: **daily/weekly**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from thousands to hundreds of thousands of daily or weekly decisions; value concentrates in large portfolios, shared services, or high-cost workflows.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: data warehouse/lakehouse, BI, planning, ERP, CRM, HRIS, external market/news data, board-reporting systems. In practice this data exists unevenly and usually needs standardization; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to prioritize, assign, inspect, refer; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are outcomes, backlog, timeliness, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium: labels, causality, process standardization, or integration are often the bottleneck. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Executive analytics cuts across functions; the material variant is whether it is used for real operating decisions or only static reporting.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to every function; cross-functional use cases synthesize decision evidence rather than own frontline execution.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in pilots but less consistently scaled: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: BCG found only 26% of companies had moved beyond proofs of concept to tangible value in 2024, and future-built companies in 2025 achieved five times the revenue increases and three times the cost reductions of others. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [BCG 2025 AI value-gap study](https://www.bcg.com/publications/2025/are-you-generating-value-from-ai-the-widening-gap)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are dashboards without owners, metric definitions that differ by function, hallucinated narrative, lack of drill-through, and executive decisions not tied to operating actions. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Scoring: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [McKinsey 2023 generative AI economic-potential report](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/tech-and-ai/our-insights/the-economic-potential-of-generative-ai-the-next-productivity-frontier); [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [BCG 2025 AI value-gap study](https://www.bcg.com/publications/2025/are-you-generating-value-from-ai-the-widening-gap); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Public records linkage

1. **Name + aliases** — Public records linkage; aliases include entity resolution, master data matching, identity linkage.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which records refer to the same person, entity, property, or event. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: public sector. Outcome owner: general counsel, legal operations, contracting, compliance, records, discovery counsel.
4. **Economic pool** — Contract and document value is leakage and labor: KPMG reported more than 9% average contract-value leakage and 4.2% of contract value spent on avoidable post-award management. [KPMG contracting-process study](https://assets.kpmg.com/content/dam/kpmg/dk/pdf/Acor/2021/07/can-the-contracting-process-improve-without-an-owner.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **record/person/entity/address**. Decision: **merge, link, dedupe, investigate**. Label/outcome: **match accuracy, fraud detection, service speed**. Horizon: **batch/daily**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from millions of real-time or daily decisions in high-volume enterprises; value concentrates in large portfolios, shared services, or high-cost workflows.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: CLM, document management, e-discovery, email, DMS, policy systems, matter management, regulatory feeds. In practice this data exists unevenly and usually needs standardization; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to merge, link, dedupe, investigate; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are match accuracy, fraud detection, service speed, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium: labels, causality, process standardization, or integration are often the bottleneck. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — High-volume contracting emphasizes extraction and playbooks; litigation emphasizes recall and privilege; regulated operations emphasize evidence, audit trail, and defensible workflow.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to finance, procurement, and compliance; legal/document use cases own interpretation, obligation, privilege, and defensibility.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in pilots but less consistently scaled: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: JPMorgan’s COiN was reported to save 360,000 hours of annual legal and loan-agreement review work; KPMG found contracting value leakage above 9%, creating a measurable target pool. [ABA Journal JPMorgan COiN report](https://www.abajournal.com/news/article/jpmorgan_chase_uses_tech_to_save_360000_hours_of_annual_work_by_lawyers_and); [KPMG contracting-process study](https://assets.kpmg.com/content/dam/kpmg/dk/pdf/Acor/2021/07/can-the-contracting-process-improve-without-an-owner.pdf)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are poor OCR, clause ambiguity, privilege risk, nonstandard templates, missing human review, and inability to prove defensible process. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Entity: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [KPMG contracting-process study](https://assets.kpmg.com/content/dam/kpmg/dk/pdf/Acor/2021/07/can-the-contracting-process-improve-without-an-owner.pdf); [ABA Journal JPMorgan COiN report](https://www.abajournal.com/news/article/jpmorgan_chase_uses_tech_to_save_360000_hours_of_annual_work_by_lawyers_and); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Real-estate / construction project risk

1. **Name + aliases** — Real-estate / construction project risk; aliases include schedule risk, cost-overrun prediction, project controls analytics.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which projects or work packages are likely to overrun budget or schedule. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: construction. Outcome owner: project executive, preconstruction, estimating, project controls, safety, risk management.
4. **Economic pool** — Capital-project risk is high value: McKinsey reported that 98% of megaprojects suffer cost overruns or delays, with average cost increases of 80% and average slippage of 20 months. [McKinsey construction productivity report](https://www.mckinsey.com/~/media/McKinsey/Industries/Capital%20Projects%20and%20Infrastructure/Our%20Insights/The%20construction%20productivity%20imperative/The%20construction%20productivity%20imperative.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **project/work package/RFI/submittal**. Decision: **escalate, resequence, staff, renegotiate**. Label/outcome: **delay, margin, change orders**. Horizon: **weekly/monthly**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from hundreds to thousands of recurring decisions; value concentrates where the entity volume and loss/revenue per decision are both high.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: project management, BIM, estimating, scheduling, RFI/submittals, ERP/job cost, safety observations, document control. In practice this data exists unevenly and usually needs standardization; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.
8. **Actionability** — A correct prediction or decision enables the owner to escalate, resequence, staff, renegotiate; actions should run through the system of action already used by the function, such as CRM, ERP, ITSM, WMS/TMS, MES, EHR, HRIS, CLM, SIEM, or a controlled workflow queue.
9. **Measurability** — Impact should be proven with A/B tests, randomized or quasi-experimental holdouts, champion/challenger monitoring, or before/after analysis only when randomized proof is impractical. Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are delay, margin, change orders, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium: labels, causality, process standardization, or integration are often the bottleneck. Minimum conditions are a named business owner, two or more cycles of history, stable label definition, governed feature access, workflow integration, and a holdout or experiment design; NIST’s AI RMF frames this as governing, mapping, measuring, and managing risks across the AI lifecycle. [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
11. **Industry variants** — Infrastructure and megaprojects emphasize schedule/cost risk; commercial construction emphasizes RFIs, submittals, safety, and estimating; real estate emphasizes demand, pricing, and asset risk.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to procurement and project finance; construction use cases own bid, project, RFI, safety, schedule, and cost-risk decisions.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in pilots but less consistently scaled: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: Autodesk reports construction risk factors such as over 70% of RFIs stemming from design or documentation issues; DPR’s Autodesk Takeoff pilot saved 3 to 4 hours of setup on a $100 million life-sciences project, but public quantified AI-specific project-risk results remain thin. [Autodesk Construction IQ risk article](https://www.autodesk.com/blogs/construction/leverage-machine-learning-reduce-risk-construction-iq/)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are fragmented project data, inconsistent RFI/submittal coding, contractor incentive conflicts, one-off project uniqueness, and late data entry. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
16. **Typical methodology** — Scoring: reusable data extraction, feature/context construction, model or rules+model decisioning, explanation, monitoring, and workflow execution.
17. **Key references** — [McKinsey construction productivity report](https://www.mckinsey.com/~/media/McKinsey/Industries/Capital%20Projects%20and%20Infrastructure/Our%20Insights/The%20construction%20productivity%20imperative/The%20construction%20productivity%20imperative.pdf); [Autodesk Construction IQ risk article](https://www.autodesk.com/blogs/construction/leverage-machine-learning-reduce-risk-construction-iq/); [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf).

### Bid optimization

1. **Name + aliases** — Bid optimization; aliases include estimating optimization, bid/no-bid, win-price optimization.
2. **Problem statement** — The business problem is which projects to bid and what price balances win probability and margin. The use case is defined at the problem layer: the enterprise must decide what to do about a recurring entity before the outcome is fully known.
3. **Who has it** — Industries: any sector where this entity and outcome recur at scale, with material variants noted below. Business function: construction. Outcome owner: project executive, preconstruction, estimating, project controls, safety, risk management.
4. **Economic pool** — Capital-project risk is high value: McKinsey reported that 98% of megaprojects suffer cost overruns or delays, with average cost increases of 80% and average slippage of 20 months. [McKinsey construction productivity report](https://www.mckinsey.com/~/media/McKinsey/Industries/Capital%20Projects%20and%20Infrastructure/Our%20Insights/The%20construction%20productivity%20imperative/The%20construction%20productivity%20imperative.pdf)
5. **Decision framing** — Entity: **bid/project/client/package**. Decision: **bid, pass, price, qualify**. Label/outcome: **win rate, margin, backlog quality**. Horizon: **per opportunity**.
6. **Decision frequency and scale** — Typical scale ranges from hundreds to thousands of recurring decisions; value concentrates in large portfolios, shared services, or high-cost workflows.
7. **Data exhaust** — Natural exhaust: project management, BIM, estimating, scheduling, RFI/submittals, ERP/job cost, safety observations, document control. In practice this data exists unevenly and usually needs standardization; the hard work is entity resolution, history depth, permissions, and workflow writeback.

Hard KPIs should be operating metrics the function already owns, not model accuracy alone. Primary KPIs are win rate, margin, backlog quality, plus unit economics, cycle time, exception rate, and risk-adjusted cost where applicable.
10. **Prerequisites & feasibility** — Medium: labels, causality, process standardization, or integration are often the bottleneck. 11. **Industry variants** — Infrastructure and megaprojects emphasize schedule/cost risk; commercial construction emphasizes RFIs, submittals, safety, and estimating; real estate emphasizes demand, pricing, and asset risk.
12. **Adjacent/overlapping use cases** — Adjacent to procurement and project finance; construction use cases own bid, project, RFI, safety, schedule, and cost-risk decisions.
13. **Prevalence & adoption** — Prevalence is common in pilots but less consistently scaled: McKinsey reported 88% of organizations regularly use AI in at least one function in 2025, but only about one-third are scaling AI enterprise-wide; Deloitte’s AI Dossier catalogs 125+ use cases across industry and function. [McKinsey State of AI 2025](https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/the-state-of-ai); [Deloitte AI Dossier](https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/what-we-do/capabilities/applied-artificial-intelligence/content/ai-use-cases.html)
14. **Documented results** — Representative results: Autodesk reports construction risk factors such as over 70% of RFIs stemming from design or documentation issues; DPR’s Autodesk Takeoff pilot saved 3 to 4 hours of setup on a $100 million life-sciences project, but public quantified AI-specific project-risk results remain thin. [Autodesk Construction IQ risk article](https://www.autodesk.com/blogs/construction/leverage-machine-learning-reduce-risk-construction-iq/)
15. **Failure modes** — Common failures are fragmented project data, inconsistent RFI/submittal coding, contractor incentive conflicts, one-off project uniqueness, and late data entry. [BCG 2024 AI adoption study](https://www.bcg.com/press/24october2024-ai-adoption-in-2024-74-of-companies-struggle-to-achieve-and-scale-value); [MIT Sloan Management Review and BCG 2020 study](https://sloanreview.mit.edu/projects/expanding-ais-impact-with-organizational-learning/); [NIST AI RMF 1.0](https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/ai/nist.ai.100-1.pdf)
